#220. Food delivery startup Wonder files for IPO as it explores its next big bite

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Food delivery startup Wonder is moving closer to becoming a publicly traded company after confidentially filing for an initial public offering (IPO). The company announced the filing on Thursday, marking a major milestone following years of fundraising, acquisitions, and expansion.

Wonder has yet to reveal how many shares it plans to make available or the expected price range.

Founded in 2018 by entrepreneur Marc Lore, Wonder has evolved from a food delivery concept into a broader food technology business. Its model goes beyond traditional delivery apps, with the company operating food hall-style locations where customers can order meals from multiple restaurant brands through a single platform. The company handles everything from meal preparation to delivery, pickup, and dine-in service.

Lore, who previously founded Jet.com and Diapers.com, has made no secret of his plans to take Wonder public. Those ambitions gained further attention in July, when the company announced it had raised more than $650 million in a funding round that valued it at around $9 billion. Investors include Accel, GV, AllianceBernstein, and ARK Invest.

Meanwhile, Wonder has been busy expanding its footprint and strengthening its business through acquisitions. The company has outlined ambitious growth plans, including a major push into Texas, where it aims to open more than 100 locations by the end of 2027.

Additionally, Wonder has acquired meal-kit company Blue Apron, restaurant delivery platform Grubhub, media company Tastemade, and kitchen robotics company Spyce. The latter acquisition gives Wonder access to automation technology that could help streamline food preparation and improve how food is served.

Wonder is also branching out with Wonder Create, its platform focused on letting food entrepreneurs and social media influencers develop new food concepts, restaurant brands, and menu items. The platform leverages AI to design and launch restaurant brands in less than a minute.

Another notable move is its $425 million partnership with DoorDash to expand its campus dining business, which operates at 450 colleges and universities and other locations.

Despite its growth, a move into the public markets will bring new challenges. Wonder is operating in an increasingly competitive food delivery landscape, going up against established names such as DoorDash and Uber Eats.

#219. Cristiano Ronaldo announces retirement from international football

The announcement by the footballing legend comes just weeks after a public spat with Portugal coach Jorge Jesus over playing time.

Cristiano Ronaldo took to social media on Thursday to announce that he had played his last game for Portugal.

"To the Portuguese people, the day has come for me to hand back the No. 7 shirt of our national team," the 41-year-old Ronaldo said in a video on his Instagram account.

"I never felt it belonged to me alone, but for 23 years I had the privilege and the responsibility of wearing it, defending it and carrying it with me into stadiums all around the world," Ronaldo said.

The former Manchester United, Real Madrid and Juventus forward represented his country 234 times, scoring 146 goals — making him the men's record-holder for ⁠international ​appearances and for international goals.

He captained Portugal ​to their first major trophy at ​Euro 2016 and led them to the 2019 and 2025 Nations League titles.

"It was not an easy decision, there are decisions that are not ​only with the head. They are also made with the heart," Ronaldo continued in the Instagram post.

"If I ever made you happy, ​if one of my goals ever made you hug someone, forget a worry or feel ⁠proud ​of our country, then it ​was all worth it."

He also thanked his family, the Portuguese people ​and the teammates, coaches and staff he worked with over his national ​team career.

The Portuguese Football Association (FPF) released a statement expressing its gratitude and respect for the player also known as "CR7."

"Cristiano Ronaldo has raised Portugal's profile and inspired generations of young people who dream of following in his footsteps; for many of us, he is the greatest player of all time!" it read.

The FPF intends to organize a tribute "in which the fans and the country can one more time applaud and celebrate a player who has given us so much."

While his long-rival Lionel Messi ended his Argentina career with a much anticipated farewell match last month, Ronaldo's final appearance may go down as Portugal's 1-0 win over Wales on September 24, depending on the nature of the tribute. Portugal coach Jorge Jesus left him on the bench for the following match and said he wouldn't start the next one.

Ronaldo responded by walking out of the Portugal training camp. He was subsequently suspended and fined by the FPF, but he had been expected to return to Portugal for the November international break.

Like Messi, Ronaldo intends to continue his club career with Al-Nassr in Saudi Arabia.

#218. Democratic socialist Nithya Raman wins Los Angeles mayoral race over incumbent Karen Bass

Major shake-up as LA councilmember, once considered long shot, ousts Democratic rival she had once endorsed

Who is Nithya Raman, Los Angeles’s next mayor?

Nithya Raman was elected to serve as the mayor of Los Angeles, defeating incumbent Karen Bass in a major political shake-up that reflects the growing dissatisfaction with the direction of the city.

The progressive urban planner had served on LA’s city council since 2020. She caused a stir when she entered the race earlier this year hours before the filing deadline – and shortly after endorsing Bass for re-election – and her campaign was once viewed as a long shot.

But she shot up in the polls over the summer and, with support from the Los Angeles chapter of the Democratic Socialists of America, frequently garnered comparisons with Zohran Mamdani, New York City’s mayor.

The Associated Press called the race for Raman on Thursday, with returns showing Raman was leading with just over 52% of the votes to Bass’s 48%.

Raman’s win came after a historic election night for Democrats across the US who won control of the House and Senate in a rebuke of Donald Trump and his party. The contest in deep blue Los Angeles was unique by comparison. Raman and Bass are progressives, and the mayoral race became a bitter battle between two candidates representing different generations and frequently clashing wings of the party.

“I love this city and I love its people. And I cannot wait to get started on building a better future together with all of you,” Raman said in a video statement after her victory.

Raman also heaped praise on her former ally Bass, describing her “someone who has given decades of her life to making the city what it is today”.

The conciliatory tone marked a departure from a contentious race, where Raman and Bass traded biting remarks about each other’s perceived failures to address the city’s woes.

Bass had never before lost an election, but faced a difficult road to winning another term as mayor given her low approval ratings on par with Donald Trump’s, Angelenos’ frustration about the state of the city, and the wave of anti-establishment sentiment that has swept US politics.

The election came as Los Angeles grappled with significant challenges, including frustration over homelessness, as well as rising costs and housing affordability.

Meanwhile, the Trump administration’s deportation campaign hit the region hard, Hollywood is struggling, and the city faces a huge budget shortfall.

“While this is not the outcome we hoped for, the people of Los Angeles have spoken,” Bass said on Thursday. “I have called councilmember Nithya Raman to congratulate her on her victory, and I have committed to ensuring a smooth and seamless transition. Moving this city forward is what Los Angeles needs from all of us.”

Raman pitched herself to voters as the candidate who can “meet this moment with honesty and urgency” while lowering rents, improving city streets, reducing homelessness and keeping Angelenos safe.

She struggled in the primary – key portions of the city’s liberal base and large unions had already aligned with Bass – and was overshadowed by Spencer Pratt’s showy campaign.

Pratt, a former reality TV star and registered Republican, decided to run after he lost his home in the Palisades fire. He had splashy and viral campaign ads critical of the mayor and Raman. He was a frequent thorn in Bass’s side, arguing she was mismanaging the city, and that officials had both failed to adequately prepare for the fire and were falling short in recovery efforts.

Raman managed to narrowly beat him in the primary, and was soon making headway. Her campaign received a significant boost from the endorsement of the Los Angeles chapter of DSA, of which she was a longtime member.

The support meant that a large network of volunteers were knocking on doors to campaign for her across the city. Shortly before the election, the organization’s national leaders considered rescinding the chapter’s support until Raman stated her position on the boycott, divestment and sanctions movement, but a vote on the matter ultimately failed.

As fall progressed, Bass’s campaign intensified its criticism of Raman, and narrowed her lead. But Raman ultimately prevailed after winning support from voters across Los Angeles.

Raman supporters celebrated and cheered during an event for the candidate outside the Greek Theatre on Tuesday night. Max Rico, a high school teacher in South Central Los Angeles, said he felt hopeful Raman would pull ahead as ballots from young voters were tallied.

Rico, who was wearing a ballot box costume from Halloween, had previously supported Bass but was left disappointed by her tenure and said her policies did not help communities that needed it. He said he was most excited about Raman’s positions on affordable housing because as a teacher he had frequently seen students leave mid-year as their families relocate to areas with lower costs of living.

“Hopefully we can keep more families here,” he said.

#217. Harbinger raises $240M after expanding into defense

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Electric truck startup Harbinger has raised a $240 million Series D funding round as it expands its portfolio of products, including battery storage and defense applications.

The funding comes almost one year to the day after the Los Angeles-based company announced a $160 million Series C, which included an initial truck order from FedEx. Harbinger has now raised more than $650 million to date, according to Bloomberg News, which first reported the round.

The Series D was co-led by Tiger Global, Capricorn’s “technology impact fund,” and defense-focused firm Ridgeline. FedEx invested again after leading last year’s round, and other repeat investors included Maniv, Greycroft, and Leitmotif, a VC firm backed by Volkswagen. Also investing this time was institutional giant Fidelity Management, which tends to invest in pre-IPO startups. (Harbinger is reportedly weighing going public.)

While it didn’t lead this round, FedEx’s relationship with Harbinger continues to deepen. The shipping giant’s senior vice president of safety and transportation, Paul Melander, has joined Harbinger’s board of directors. FedEx also recently placed its first large order with Harbinger — a deal for 2,000 trucks valued at more than $300 million.

Founded in 2022 by former employees of now-defunct EV startup Canoo and battery company QuantumScape, Harbinger started by focusing on making one electric truck chassis. The company has steadily expanded its efforts since. It bought autonomy startup Phantom AI, has begun developing hybrid emergency vehicles, and revealed a driverless hybrid electric platform for the military.

#215. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

#214. Nasdaq sees worst day in 18 months as investors bet on AI slowdown after Democrats’ election triumph

Nearly $2tn shaved off Nasdaq value as worries of curbs to AI datacenters ripple across bourses

The Nasdaq share index had its worst day in 18 months after slumping 5.5% on Thursday amid growing signs that the artificial intelligence industry will face tougher regulation because of the midterm election results.

As Wall Street sustained widespread losses, the Nasdaq composite tumbled into “correction territory” – more than 10% below its recent record high – with more than $2tn shaved off its value by the end of trading Thursday. The tech-heavy index had its biggest one-day fall since Donald Trump announced his “liberation day” tariffs on US trading partners in April 2025.

Worries over massive AI spending gripped the New York stock market and rippled out to other bourses worldwide as investors anticipated tougher scrutiny of datacentre expansion plans.

Chip stocks led the slump, with memory maker SanDisk tumbling by 22% and hard drive maker Western Digital falling by 17%.

Wall Street’s “fear index”, the VIX, jumped by 61% to its highest levels since the early weeks of the Iran war, in a sign of rising market volatility.

Analysts pointed to expectations that Democratic governors will clamp down on the rollout of new datacentres after the party won a series of gubernatorial races this week. US voters also rejected datacentres in a series of local ballots across the country.

Neil Wilson, investor strategist at Saxo UK, said a “complete capitulation-level event” was playing out in tech stocks on Thursday.

“The fear is a total momentum unwind as Democrats pace the frontier and kibosh datacentre buildout … this looks totally overdone but the nature of ‘sell everything’ in this market means we need to look for some stability first,” Wilson said.

The Dow Jones industrial average (DJIA) of 30 large US companies was down more than 1,800 points by the end of the day, a fall of 3.4%, to 51,868 points. The S&P 500 was down 4.3%.

Nvidia, the chipmaker at the heart of the AI boom, was the top faller on the DJIA – down 9.2%, followed by Goldman Sachs, which fell 3.7%.

An early stock market rally in Europe had fizzled out by late trading, with Britain’s FTSE 100 closing down 46 points or 0.4% at 10782.66.

The sell-off is a sharp reverse of Wednesday’s rally, when Wall Street stocks had their biggest daily gain in months as investors anticipated that the Democratic party’s success in the US midterm elections could restrain the White House.

That rally prompted Donald Trump to once again boast about the record-breaking highs the stock markets had reached during his second term. The president wrote on Truth Social: “Stock Market just hit another Record High – 91 Days in just a short period of time — Less than two years. Your 401(k)s are through the roof. Record Setting All! President DONALD J. TRUMP.”

Tech analyst Dan Ives has warned that the results of the midterms are a clear sign that the pace of datacentre buildout will start to slow down.

“There is no way to sugarcoat that Democrats taking control, especially over the Senate, will create some major hurdles for AI and tech legislation in the Beltway over the next two years,” Ives said.

“The market’s moving from the promise ⁠of AI to the price of AI,” said Ted Parkhill, founder and CEO of Incline ​Investment Management.

“There’s no question that AI is ​going to be transformative for the world in ​the long run. But that doesn’t mean every AI-related stock is currently priced appropriately. There’s going to be ​a lot of turmoil.”

#213. Claude can now analyze your spending with its new Personal Finance tool

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Anthropic announced Thursday that Claude is getting a new tool called “Personal Finance,” designed to help users keep track of their money. With it, users can connect their bank and credit card accounts through Plaid and ask Claude questions about their finances. Anthropic says access is read-only, meaning Personal Finance can’t move, manage, or invest users’ money.

By letting Claude connect directly to bank and credit card accounts, Anthropic is positioning its chatbot as a financial companion that can help people understand their spending and make sense of their money, rather than simply offering them generic advice.

With the launch, Claude is playing catch-up to OpenAI’s ChatGPT, which introduced similar personal finance tools in May that also let users connect their financial accounts and ask questions about everything from spending patterns to long-term financial planning.

“Claude gives you answers in everyday language, from your balances and transactions, and explains any term that’s new to you,” Anthropic wrote in a blog post. “Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations, and it doesn’t offer credit cards, loans, or savings accounts of its own, so your money questions get a straight answer.”

For instance, at the end of the month, a user could ask Claude, “Where did my money go this month?” The chatbot will then add up spending across the connected accounts and sort it by category, such as rent and utilities, food and drink, and shopping. Users can choose to go deeper by following up with questions like, “Is that more than usual?” or “Anything else I’m missing?”

Anthropic says the tool goes beyond basic spending summaries by analyzing transactions across connected accounts to flag potential issues, such as duplicate charges, unexpected fees, and steadily increasing bills. Users can also ask broader questions about their finances, such as whether they’re spending more than they earn. The tool can also explain financial concepts like APR (annual percentage rate), while providing calculations that show users how much they’ve paid in interest over the past year.

Of course, some people will have reservations about connecting their financial accounts to an AI chatbot. Anthropic says Claude’s access is limited to each connected bank’s name, account name and type, the last four digits of the account number, balances, credit limits, and up to two years of transaction history.

Additionally, the company notes that when users chat with Claude in the Personal Finance hub, those conversations aren’t used to train its models.

Personal Finance starts rolling out in beta this week to users on the Pro, Max, and Ultra plans in the U.S.

The launch of the new tool comes as Anthropic has been expanding Claude into new verticals. For instance, Anthropic launched Claude for Legal and Claude for Financial Services earlier this year.

#212. Pioneering Jewish academy opens in Germany

A Jewish academy has opened its doors in Frankfurt. The Central Council of Jews in Germany, the country’s leading Jewish umbrella group, set up the project to combat hate and promote dialogue and learning nationally.

The western city of Frankfurt was a very conscious choice of location for the new academy, which is the first of its kind in Germany. Frankfurt has traditionally been a hub of Jewish intellectual life — still one of the most important centers of Jewish life in Germany today alongside Berlin, Munich and Düsseldorf.

Less than a 10-minute walk from the city's biggest synagogue, the Jewish Academy is also within sight of the historic festival hall building on Frankfurt Trade Fair grounds where the Nazis herded Jewish men in 1938 and deported them to concentration camps.

It was also here that the Central Council of Jews in Germany was founded in 1950; Berlin serves as its headquarters today. Frankfurt is also home to the Central Welfare Board of Jews in Germany (ZWST) and a Jewish museum that opened in 1988.

Jewish thinkers have played a key role in Frankfurt's intellectual and academic history. The city's Jewish community contributed to the establishment of Goethe University in 1914.

Another important legacy is the Jewish Lehrhaus (house of study). Founded in 1920, it quickly gained prominence and is today associated with the philosopher Franz Rosenzweig (1886–1929). It was closed by the Nazis in 1938. The new academy sees itself in the tradition of Rosenzweig's Lehrhaus.

Speaking at the opening ceremony on Monday, Josef Schuster, president of the Central Council of Jews in Germany, stressed: "We must combat hatred with education, education and more education."

Schuster said the institute was a "milestone” that represented the "Jewish community's determination to call Germany home." At the same time, he emphasized: "Jewish life in Germany is under greater threat than it has been since the crime against humanity that was the Shoah."

German President Frank-Walter Steinmeier also underlined the need to reject antisemitism "outright and without reservation." He added that it must also be combated politically.

The federal president said the murder of six million Jews in the Holocaust had also targeted the Jewish world of learning and knowledge. He specifically honored Rosenzweig's legacy: "This place of knowledge and dialogue was a source of hope, something exciting and something new."

The Jewish Academy combines traditional features of a 110-year-old turn-of-the-century German villa with a four-story modern structure, bringing together old and new, history and modernity.

"We see ourselves as a place where the past, present and future are all explored in equal measure," Sabena Donath, the institution's director, told DW.

Donath and her co-director, Doron Kiesel, view the academy as "a bridge between academia and the general public" and as "a home for scholars and a home for those who ask questions."

Donath said the aim was to put the focus on topics related to the philosophy of religion and ethics as well as on topical debates concerning contemporary Jewish life.

The program aims to reflect the many facets of Jewish life and the diverse forms of Judaism.

Kiesel told DW: "We want to show how Jews live today and how heterogeneous Jewish life is. And that it is completely wrong to speak of 'the' Jews or 'the' Jewish way of thinking."

Ahead of the opening, the president of the Central Council of Jews also named interfaith dialogue as one of the institution's topics, and, in an interview with the German newspaper, the Frankfurter Allgemeine, Schuster expressed the hope that the academy would bring into the public sphere "Jewish themes besides the topic of antisemitism and Jewish persecution.”

But these issues will also be on the agenda. "We have,” said Kiesel, "a problem in this country that antisemitism is concealed behind numerous topics, but keeps on resurfacing, sometimes more evidently, sometimes more subtly.”

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The project grew out of an initiative from the former head of the Central Council Dieter Graumann, who encouraged the setting up of an educational department in 2012. Its work was shaped by the directors of the new institution.

A mezuzah — a small capsule containing verses from the Torah intended to serve as a reminder of God's commandments — is affixed to the entrance of the academy. Security measures are also an integral part of this new building.

"It is a bitter reality that Jewish institutions must be guarded," said Donath. The academy is being protected just as Jewish schools and synagogues must be protected. "For us, this is a sad reality of everyday life."

The construction costs of around €40 million ($45 million) — about half of which was covered by the Central Council of Jews and half by the federal and state governments — ended up being significantly higher than calculated at the groundbreaking in 2021, partly due to the structural security measures.

#210. China Renaissance suspends trading, delays results after founder's disappearance

China Renaissance, a top dealmaker in the country’s tech industry, said it would suspend trading of its shares and delay the release of its annual results because it still can’t get in touch with its founder.

Bao Fan, 52, started the boutique investment bank in 2005 and has been unreachable since the middle of February, according to the company. Shares in China Renaissance have plunged since Bao went missing, at one point dropping as much as 50%.

China Renaissance said in late February that it had learned Bao was “cooperating in an investigation” being carried out by certain authorities in the country. It gave no other details.

Chinese media have reported Bao might be assisting in an investigation related to a former executive at China Renaissance.

In a filing on Sunday, China Renaissance said auditors couldn’t complete their work or sign off on their report because of Bao’s absence. The board was also unable to give an estimate about when it would be able to approve its audited results for 2022 or dispatch its annual report by an April 30 deadline as required by Hong Kong’s listing rules.

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

Trading in the company’s shares was suspended from Monday as a result.

Bao is known as a veteran dealmaker who works closely with top technology companies in China. He helped broker the 2015 merger between two of the country’s leading food delivery services, Meituan and Dianping. Today, the combined company’s “super app” platform is ubiquitous in China.

His team has also invested in US-listed Chinese electric vehicle makers Nio (NIO) and Li Auto and helped Chinese internet giants Baidu (BIDU) and JD.com (JD) complete their secondary listings in Hong Kong.

Over the weekend, China’s top anti-graft watchdog launched an investigation into Liu Liange, former party secretary and chairman of Bank of China, according to a statement by the Central Commission for Discipline Inspection and the State Supervision Commission. The bank is state-owned and one of the country’s four biggest lenders.

Liu is suspected of “serious violations of discipline and law,” the statement said. He is among the most senior financial executives targeted in a broader financial crackdown by President Xi Jinping.

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#209. Salon, internet’s oldest general interest publication, shuts down after 31 years

Website known for politics, arts and culture coverage announces closure just weeks before anniversary

Salon, one of the early internet’s pioneering publications, is closing after more than three decades, the outlet announced on Thursday.

“It’s difficult to find the right words for the end of something that has meant so much to so many people. Salon was never just a website,” Salon said in a statement. “It was an idea about what journalism could be, born at a moment when the internet was still a largely unexplored frontier.”

The site, which built its reputation from politics, arts and culture coverage, announced its closure just weeks before its anniversary on 20 November.

“The business realities of independent digital journalism have changed profoundly over the years. After careful consideration, we have reached the difficult decision that our publishing model is no longer sustainable. We know this news will be deeply disappointing to many of you, and we share that sadness,” the statement said.

Founded in San Francisco in 1995, Salon said its output “helped pioneer a form of journalism that would come to define an entirely new era of publishing.”

Salon, which moved most editorial staff to New York City in the 2000s, was described as the oldest general interest publication on the internet, according to a piece by the Columbia Journalism Review in 2011.

“We are profoundly grateful to the journalists, editors, photographers, producers, designers, business teams and countless other colleagues who brought this publication to life across its many chapters,” the Salon statement said.

The publication won a plethora of awards for its work through the 1990s, including 1996’s website of the year from Time magazine and 1997’s best website from BusinessWeek.

“Thirty-one years is a remarkable run,” Salon said. “What matters most is what happened during those years: the journalism, the ideas, the voices, the conversations and the community that grew around them.”

#208. The ex-Secret Service agent who flipped a hacker is now raising a second $250M cybersecurity fund

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Every time venture capitalists write a check, they’re secretly running numbers in their heads about different exit scenarios. It’s their job as fiduciaries. But nearly all will tell you (publicly, anyway) that every portfolio company is headed for an IPO.

Stephen Ward, the co-founder of Brightmind Partners, a two-year-old cybersecurity fund in New York, says otherwise outright. “You have to sell on the way up,” he says.

Ward’s unvarnished pragmatism is one reason he stands out. Another is the size of his firm’s debut fund late last year: a whopping $250 million fund, which he closed with co-founder Gur Talpaz. A third reason is his resume, which includes a stint with the Coast Guard, nine years in the Secret Service, and CISO jobs at TIAA and Home Depot before he ever thought about investing.

One proof point that suggests the combination is working is SGNL, the identity security startup CrowdStrike agreed in January to buy for $740 million, 11 months after Brightmind led its Series A.

Now, Ward is raising a second fund, targeting roughly the same size as the first one. This time, though, he’ll be doing it without Talpaz, who left in April along with another early colleague, leaving Ward as the firm’s sole general partner. He’s betting on himself at a moment when early-stage cybersecurity startups are raising more money than later-stage rivals for the first time in more than a decade.

His unemotional approach to exits traces back to an unlikely career. Ward says he grew up in northwest New Jersey between two farms, in a family that couldn’t afford college for his older brothers. After high school, he joined the Coast Guard, inspired by a commercial of a man jumping out of a helicopter, though he says he barely knew how to swim at the time. He spent four years in search and rescue.

Afterward, he studied criminal justice at Seton Hall in New Jersey and thought half-heartedly about law school. “I didn’t know what I wanted to do,” Ward told me last week over Zoom, wearing a pale hooded sweatshirt and looking more like a basketball coach than a venture capitalist. “I was kind of lost. I missed the military and the camaraderie and all of that.”

After graduating in 1998, he says, he mailed out some 300 resumes but no one responded. A Seton Hall professor who was also a chaplain to Secret Service personnel, as well as to members of the FBI and CIA, began introducing him around, and the Secret Service called first.

The Secret Service has two primary missions: protecting people and investigating financial crimes. Ward gravitated to investigations, then to “cyber” after the September 11 attacks in 2001. That year, he married a former college classmate, an American-born Syrian Muslim. Colleagues warned him the background check would be “miserable.” (They’re still happily married.)

What came next took Ward’s life down an unexpected path. Stationed in New York, he arrested a hacker who’d pulled off the largest credit card data theft of that era. Instead of charging him, however, Ward flipped him, turning him into an informant and running him for about three years. But the hacker kept hacking behind Ward’s back, and after he was caught again, he told the agency, as a diversionary tactic, that Ward was in on it.

By that time, Ward had left for the private sector, and he ultimately spent roughly 18 months clearing his name. “It was hard to get out of bed,” he said of that period, describing the investigation as “almost like a spouse cheating on you,” given his love of the agency. The hacker ultimately received a 20-year sentence, and Audible bought the rights to the story, turning it into an eight-episode podcast called “Operation Firewall.”

Ward’s corporate career has been a success by most measures, though he describes it as a series of jumps he really wasn’t qualified to make. His first private-sector job at J.P. Morgan, where he was recruited in 2007 to build a cybersecurity unit, was the hardest transition of his career, he says. He felt like “the dumbest person in the room,” missed his Secret Service crew, and twice thought about going back, unaware he was still under investigation. He stuck it out and now calls the investment bank the “best management school.”

Six years as CISO at the financial services company TIAA followed, and then two years at Home Depot starting in 2019. But in 2021, feeling burned out, he quit without anything lined up for the first time since age 16.

That chapter didn’t last long. The founders of Wiz, whose security software Ward had used at Home Depot, put him on a short list for a job at their company. Jeff Horing, a venture capitalist at Insight Partners, which had co-led Wiz’s $100 million Series A round in late 2020, interviewed Ward for the role, then offered him one at Insight instead.

It’s a good-news, bad-news story, given that Wiz was acquired last year by Google for $32 billion. When I asked him about it last week, Ward observed that the friend who took the Wiz job “made life-changing money,” but said it doesn’t bother him.

Regardless, he spent just three years at Insight, where he says he worked on Wiz, Abnormal Security, and Elisity, which IonQ agreed in October to buy for up to $1.3 billion. Then, at age 49, he thought about striking out on his own for the first time.

He says people told him it was a young man’s game. He left anyway and began cold-emailing limited partners. Ultimately, fewer than 20 LPs filled the fund, including Horing.

Ward started Brightmind in 2024 with Talpaz, a CrowdStrike corporate development executive whose deals overlapped with Ward’s during his time at Insight. Tayler Sipperly, another CrowdStrike corporate development alum, was the firm’s third employee.

The partnership ended this past spring. Asked what happened, Ward, using carefully worded legalese, cited “differences in opinions regarding operations and the future of Brightmind.” In August, Talpaz and Sipperly co-founded a new venture firm with Elia Zaitsev, CrowdStrike’s global CTO for the past three years. Called Cognition, it’s now reportedly targeting a $170 million first fund to back cybersecurity startups.

Brightmind’s first fund has made 14 investments to date, with one exit: that SNGL sale.

Some investors asked why Brightmind didn’t ride out that bet even longer. But Ward says he’s seen enough across his career that he feels confident in warning founders when they’re “going to race past the turn.”

In cybersecurity, he says, the best sales come between a startup’s third and seventh years. Indeed, he says his team tracks every security acquisition and what buyers are building versus buying. If Palo Alto Networks is likely to buy something, he says, CrowdStrike will experience “FOMO, then Zscaler is going to look over and go, ‘What did they just do? I should probably do the same thing.’”

Understanding how the pieces fit together is the trick, Ward explained, offering some hypotheticals. If CrowdStrike is building a product, he said, it’s unlikely to buy a portfolio company that makes something similar. Palo Alto Networks might love a company, but not if buying it would force its customers to switch away from something it bought five years ago.

“It’s very easy to go, ‘Good match,’ ‘terrible match,’” he said. “And that’s really what we spend a ton of time figuring out.”

He has timing on his side, at least. Early-stage cybersecurity startups are out-raising their later-stage rivals for the first time in more than a decade, according to PitchBook, which counts $4.7 billion worth of seed-through-Series B deals through September.

That total is already ahead of the entirety of 2025, even though the number of deals fell to 248 from 358 last year. Seed rounds in particular are ballooning, per PitchBook. The average cybersecurity seed round jumped to $8.4 million in the first nine months of this year, from $5.54 million a year earlier.

Ward questions how much of that money is really going to AI security. By PitchBook’s count, 278 deals in the first half of 2026 were bucketed as such, but his team’s manual review found only 30 true ones. He doesn’t think Anthropic or OpenAI will “take over the security world,” but instead will do “just enough.”

For real-time protection, which he terms “zero second,” he favors cyber-specific models that run locally on a customer’s own systems, like that of Empirical Security. He also looks for startups that could trounce established companies that he considers vulnerable, name-checking 27-year-old Qualys and 24-year-old Tenable.

Ward says Brightmind’s second fund won’t be entirely cybersecurity, focusing about 20% on AI infrastructure deals. He expects a meaningful number of teams to come from Israel, too. (Roughly 40% of the debut fund’s startups are based in Tel Aviv.)

Asked about the founders he’s most comfortable backing, the response is immediate: second-time founders who stick together.

Brightmind is comprised of five people right now: COO Iva Messy, also a Seton Hall graduate, came from venture firms Felicis and Andreessen Horowitz (a16z) on the recommendation of Ward’s cousin, Carl Eschenbach, a former Workday CEO who is now back at Sequoia. Sven Wollschlaeger, a Midwesterner whom Ward calls one of the hardest-working people he’s known, spent three years alongside Ward at Insight, and is a principal at Brightmind.

CTO Jamie Mneimneh, whom Ward first hired at Home Depot as a distinguished engineer, is the most technical person of the team. He built the cloud computing environment on AWS where the firm tests startups’ products. Mneimneh also built the AI agents that now source most of Brightmind’s deal flow, which is how the firm avoids hiring a bench of analysts and keeps management fees down, says Ward.

Taylor Cleveland, Ward’s chief of staff at Home Depot, joined the firm a few months ago.

For all the career changes, Ward says the real force driving him hasn’t changed. He grew up without money and still worries it could happen again, saying he’s being paid to produce a “3x net fund,” and he’s “not going to celebrate until we get that.”

Said Ward: “I don’t know what’s wrong with me. I have a level of being very naive to like, not being qualified to do something. I’ve just always looked at people and been like, ‘Okay, you know more than me today, but tomorrow, it’s going to be a little less, and the day after that, it’s going to be less, and eventually, I’m going to catch you and surpass you, and I’m going to know more than you.’ That’s how competitive I am.”

#206. What does Trump's midterm elections loss mean for strained US-India ties?

Donald Trump's electoral setback might give New Delhi and Washington space to reset relations, but there is no guarantee that the US will actually change its approach to India.

With the US Democrats scoring a resounding midterm victory and taking control of both chambers of Congress, the Republican administration led by Donald Trump is set for a new level of scrutiny until the end of Trump's term.

Trump is likely to face pushback on his controversial foreign policy, which saw the US alienate most of its allies and get entangled in yet another unpopular war in the Middle East. At the same time, the Trump administration has also taken a slew of less conspicuous but still highly controversial moves on the global stage, including a series of confrontations with India.

The ties between New Delhi and Washington are still strained by a stalled trade agreement, restrictions on immigration, and disagreement over India buying Russian oil and weapons, despite the US granting an oil ban waiver in March 2026 and Trump waving some of the Russia sanctions in October.

And while the new US Congress can choose to pressure Trump over issues such as tariffs and immigration, the president still wields significant authority on foreign policy.

The immediate test for Trump's India policy is trade. India and the US have yet to formalize the interim framework discussed by Trump and Prime Minister Narendra Modi in February, with the two nations still at odds over market access and other trade commitments.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Shanthie Mariet D'Souza, founder of the Mantraya Institute of Strategic Studies, expects the new Congress to apply "increased scrutiny of protectionist measures" pushed by the Trump administration.

The US lawmakers could push for "stronger trade oversight and clearer standards for environmental and labor issues," she told DW.

For India, whose largest export market is the US, this could offer some breathing room.

She also emphasized that "the fundamental pillars of the India-US strategic partnership are expected to remain strong" and "the long-term goal of deepening economic ties is likely to continue."

Immigration presents a different challenge. The administration's tightening of H-1B visa rules, including imposing a $100,000 (€88,761) payment for certain new petitions, has alarmed Indian professionals and tech companies that rely on the visa program to secure jobs in the US.

A Democratic majority could increase scrutiny on that issue as well, but would not automatically reverse measures introduced by Trump's executive action.

"Both parties have bigger issues to grapple with," said Rajkrishna Iyer, a California-licensed immigration lawyer. "For the last 25-plus years, Congress has done nothing for immigration," he added.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Iyer believes that legal immigration itself is not a major priority for voters. Nevertheless, he sees the midterms' result as a message to Trump about his immigration policies.

"A rational president would not spend time imposing further restrictions on legal immigration. But as we know, the president is not rational," Iyer said.

The immigration lawyer believes that new, steep fee for some H-1B applications will be challenged in court.

"Most likely, the new rules will not come into effect. And if they do, federal courts are likely to strike them down because the fees are not reasonable," said Iyer.

While the executive branch of government can raise fees to reflect administrative costs and inflation, Iyer argued that charges of this magnitude could deter people from pursuing legal immigration. "Only Congress can pass such legislation."

The new balance of power in Washington could also trigger a new geopolitical reshuffle and a change of course on Russia. However, it is unclear if the Democrat-dominated Congress will force any changes in the dispute between New Delhi and Washington over India's purchasing Russian energy. During a congressional vote on Russian sanctions in September, a substantial number of Democrats joined Republican lawmakers to endorse trade restrictions against Moscow.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"Regarding Russian oil, the administration is likely to maintain its preference for reducing dependence on Moscow, but it will approach this issue with diplomatic caution to avoid straining relations with New Delhi," D'Souza said.

The war in Iran adds another dimension. Any new escalation could affect energy exports from the Middle East and make India more vulnerable due to its need for high oil imports.

But Navtej Sarna, a former Indian ambassador to the US and Israel, urged cautions when discussing the future of US policy in the Middle East.

"It may be facile to try and parse these results for their immediate impact on specific areas of foreign policy. Much will depend on how Trump reacts," Sarna told DW.

At the same time, Sarna expects Washington to become "a more contested place," with greater congressional pushback on issues that divide India and the US.

"If that injects some balance and stability and restores institutional linkages it's good for the long-term future of our relationship," he added.

Security cooperation is another stumbling block. New Delhi is still by far the biggest buyer of Russia's arms exports, which irritates Washington. At the same time, India has objected to US supplying weapons to Pakistan, including the $686 million sale of advanced military tech for Pakistan's F-16 jets.

"Washington's military support for Pakistan remains a complex issue. However, ongoing regional adjustments and US concerns about stability in the region will probably prevent any significant policy changes," said D'Souza.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The broader question is whether the election produces a more predictable Washington or a more confrontational Donald Trump.

A weakened president could be more inclined to seek compromises at home. But Trump could also respond to congressional resistance by taking a harder line elsewhere.

"We hope the electoral setback will prompt Trump to undertake a more considered review of his earlier actions," Amitabh Mattoo, dean of the School of International Studies at Jawaharlal Nehru University, told DW.

But he also warns that it too early to assess the implications for India-US relations.

"Rather than moderating his approach, the president could double down and adopt an even harder line towards New Delhi if he comes to believe that Indians, particularly sections of the diaspora, played a role, directly or indirectly, in his party's defeat."

Harsh Pant of the Observer Research said that the US president retains considerable leverage over foreign policy despite the Republican setback, and that the India-US partnership has traditionally enjoyed bipartisan support.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"I don't think India will be one of those issues" on which the White House and Congress are likely to clash, Pant told DW.

But the experience of the past two years, he argues, reinforces the need for India to reduce its dependence on any single partner.

"The need for diversification of partnerships, the need to be self-reliant in certain critical sectors, the need to have alternative supply chains — all of those issues remain where they are. The US midterms do not really change the calculus," he said.

#205. Fox News suffers ratings drop on bleak election night for Republicans

News channel trailed NBC and ABC, but edged out CNN, MS Now and CBS in primetime hours on midterm election night

On a midterm election night that was a huge disappointment for Republicans, with Democrats taking control of the US House and Senate, the country’s most prominent conservative television network seemingly felt the brunt of viewer disillusionment with Donald Trump and his party, according to final audience numbers released on Thursday.

The Fox News channel, which often leads among all news networks in viewership on election nights, fell behind two top broadcasters on Tuesday. According to ratings dating from the Nielsen firm, the network averaged 4.3 million total viewers between 8 and 11pm, behind broadcast networks NBC (6.7 million viewers) and ABC (5.2 million viewers), though it edged out fellow cable networks CNN (4.1 million viewers) and MS Now (3.7 million viewers). Fox News handedly defeat CBS News, which attracted just 2.8 million viewers. CNN performed better in the 25-to-54 age demographic prized by advertisers, besting Fox News, MS Now and CBS.

Originally, preliminary viewership numbers released Wednesday put Fox News behind both CNN and MS Now in total viewership in primetime.

Overall, the big six television networks averaged nearly 27 million total viewers during prime-time election coverage, up from 22.215 million total viewers in 2022. During those 2022 elections, Fox News averaged 7.422 million viewers, meaning its viewership slipped about 41% on Tuesday.

With Trump reaching historic levels of unpopularity, Fox News has seen some viewers tune out in recent weeks. Last month, the network’s viewership was down 27% compared with the same month last year in total day viewers, though it still handily defeated CNN and MS Now. Lachlan Murdoch, the chief executive of parent company Fox Corp, acknowledged “viewer fatigue” in an earnings call on Monday.

Trump, who criticized Fox throughout election night and blamed the network for his party’s losses, poked fun at the network’s October figures in a Truth Social post, chalking up the decline to the channel’s industry-standard practice of booking Democratic candidates for interviews.

“FoxNews Ratings are way down (27%!) because they’re spending too much time covering the Trump Deranged Dumocrats!” he wrote on Tuesday evening. “All of those people who used to watch Fox, and no longer do, get it. It’s them, not me – My numbers are better than they have ever been.”

This story was updated on Thursday afternoon to reflect final viewership figures released by Nielsen.

#204. OpenAI president Greg Brockman says company could be IPO ready in 2027

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

OpenAI co-founder and president Greg Brockman said the company would be ready for an IPO in 2027, noting that its enterprise revenue has been growing. Speaking at a Wall Street Journal tech conference on Wednesday, the executive touted how the company had been gaining ground in the enterprise market, where rival Anthropic had already successfully made inroads.

In response to the interviewer’s question about being IPO-ready next year, Brockman replied, “I think so. Yes.”

“We’ve been very clear this year that we want to go public when we feel is the right time for us, and I feel extremely good about the business. I feel like we’ve made so much progress this year, and I’ve honestly never felt more confident in what we’re doing,” Brockman said.

He detailed how OpenAI’s trajectory as an enterprise had changed over the course of 2026, noting that the first and second quarters were “pretty rough.” He also admitted that OpenAI “wasn’t even in the conversation” when it came to agentic coding — an area where Anthropic’s Claude Code has gained market share.

“We had Codex, but no one was talking about it,” Brockman continued, “and I think that we just kept our heads down and executed.”

By the third quarter, activity picked up, as OpenAI saw 70% quarterly growth in its annualized revenue — something Brockman attributed to the enterprise side of the business doubling. He explained that the growth wasn’t simply the result of something that happened in the quarter itself, but rather the culmination of investments the company had been making up to that point.

Now the majority of OpenAI’s revenue is coming from the enterprise business, which previously accounted for only around 30% of its total revenue.

“And it’s not that consumer [revenue has shrunk] … It’s been growing, but it’s the enterprise segment has really taken off,” he said, pointing to a recent partnership with JPMorgan Chase as one example of the type of client it’s now winning deals with.

Brockman also spoke more broadly about the lessons OpenAI learned from selling into the enterprise market as well. He said the company had come to understand that its job was to help businesses identify the most important milestones they could achieve with its AI technology, build trust with clients, and demonstrate that it was a reliable partner for the long haul.

“A year ago, we weren’t even really in the enterprise game, right? We were really perceived as a consumer company, and a lot of the companies we’d start talking to would say, ‘Is OpenAI really committed to the enterprise?’” Brockman said. “But it’s so different today.”

#201. US government warns of ‘ongoing cyber threat’ amid mass hacks of industrial retail and military freezers

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

The U.S. government is warning of an “ongoing cyber threat” facing industrial refrigeration units, after reports of cyberattacks targeting freezers and fridges in supermarkets around the world, U.S. military bases, and at least one blood bank.

U.S. cybersecurity agency CISA, the FBI, and several other federal government departments warned in a joint statement on Thursday that hackers are targeting and compromising internet-connected refrigeration and cold chain monitoring systems in grocery stores and cold storage facilities. The statement said that the hacks are hitting critical infrastructure, such as the food, agriculture, transportation, and medicine-shipping industries.

“If successfully exploited, the actors could change the temperature and possibly cause physical impacts such as fire hazards, loss of inventory and changes to the service life of the system,” the agencies warn.

The warning comes after several waves of cyberattacks targeting industrial refrigeration units across the United Kingdom and Europe in recent weeks. Two separate waves of attacks allowed hackers to crank up the temperature on freezers and chilling units in at least a dozen countries, spoiling defrosted food and destroying produce. Some of the affected systems included remote-controlled refrigeration units made by Danish company Danfoss, which had stopped providing security updates to its customers’ freezers.

Hackers also targeted a blood bank in France, causing its refrigerated units to boost temperatures in its cold units containing donated blood supplies — but were stopped before the attack could compromise blood stores. The U.S. Department of Defense is investigating a spate of hacks targeting freezers run by the military, according to Reuters. The hacks were first reported by Signal and Silence.

The U.S. has not attributed the hacks to a particular group or government, but security researchers have broadly attributed the attacks to Iran; U.S. officials also reportedly suspect Iran is the likely culprit.

CISA, the FBI, and the Departments of Agriculture, Health and Human Services, and Transportation urged affected industries to remove remote control systems from the internet, change and strengthen passwords, apply patches where possible, and retire equipment that’s no longer supported by the vendor.

In a separate fact sheet, the departments said that hackers may attempt to “force units into continuous heating and defrost cycles, which can cause widespread inventory loss and equipment hazards.”

“Every organization that depends on refrigeration and monitoring systems should assume it could be a target,” said CISA’s acting cybersecurity chief Chris Butera.

#200. US elections 2026 live: Democrats hold nearly 100 more House seats than Republicans as blue wave continues to grow

Democrats widen gap in House chamber by flipping more seats, including in Ohio, Arizona and North Carolina; Ted Cruz calls election a ‘Watergate-level repudiation’

The Democrats have flipped another five House seats, extending their lead over the Republicans in the lower chamber 252 to 155, with 28 races left to call. Here are the latest results:

New Jersey’s second congressional district: Cape May mayor Zack Mullock, a Democrat, defeated four-term incumbent Jeff Van Drew, a Republican who was first elected as a Democrat in 2018 before switching parties after voting against the first impeachment of Donald Trump in 2019.

Arizona’s sixth congressional district: US marine veteran Democrat JoAnna Mendoza beat two-term Republican incumbent Juan Ciscomani.

Colorado’s fifth congressional district: Democrat Jessica Killin, a former Army captain, beat first-term incumbent, Republican Jeff Crank, who was criticized by relatives of Virginia Giuffre for refusing to meet them.

North Carolina’s ninth congressional district: Democrat Richard Ojeda beat two-term Republican incumbent Richard Hudson in a seat that has been red for the last 14 years.

Ohio’s 10th congressional district: Democrat Kristina Knickerbocker beat veteran Republican congressman Mike Turner.

As reporters were being hustled out of the Oval Office on Thursday, and the White House live feed cut out, Donald Trump was asked if the proclamation he signed, “to preserve the Oval Office as a national monument” would prevent his successor from undoing the gilded decor he has added to it.

“Does the next president get to take down this gold stuff?” the reporter asked.

“He can do whatever he wants but very carefully,” Trump replied. “He’s got to be very careful.”

The exchange was captured on video posted by a correspondent for the far-right, partisan network Real America’s Voice.

A spokesman for the US state department on Thursday called reports that Marco Rubio, the secretary of state, is thinking about resigning “completely baseless fake news”.

The spokesman, Dylan Johnson, a former Trump campaign official who previously served in the White House communications office, rejected reports from both the Independent and Bloomberg News that Rubio is considering stepping down ahead of a potential run for the 2028 Republican presidential nomination.

“This is complete and total fake news based on the word of anonymous sources, befitting a tabloid,” Johnson told Bloomberg.

Harmony, 24, is a far-right media figure who was hired by the White House last month despite having posed for a holiday photograph in 2021 wearing a “Fuck Joe Biden” T-shirt and holding a rifle.

Van Drew was first elected to congress as a Democrat in the 2018 midterms, defected to the Republican party after voting against the impeachment of Donald Trump in 2019 and was re-elected three times as a Republican before being toppled by Mullock in the South Jersey district.

And I could even say it might involve all of Europe. It’s going to be a big, beautiful agreement and everyone’s going to be thrilled.”

Asked by another reporter if he met with anyone from the Ukrainian delegation, Trump replied: “No, just with the head person of Russia.”

Having apparently not gotten the message from voters, or Barack Obama, that his obsessive focus on transforming the White House into a version of Mar-a-Lago is deeply unpopular, Donald Trump on Thursday took action: signing an executive order declaring the gilded Oval Office and redecorated Cabinet Room “a national monument”, which, he said, “will make it impossible for anybody ever to destroy this incredible space”.

“I just thought it was very important that nobody be allowed to come in and take it down and, try and do something with it” Trump said. The landmark status, he said, would make it “in theory, impossible to knock down, change in any meaningful way”.

The executive action by Trump suggests that he is determined not to accept the criticism from Obama at campaign rallies last month that the president is more focused on home improvement projects than finding solutions to the high prices that plague Americans.

“He’s got the White House looking like Saddam Hussein opened a Cheesecake Factory,” Obama told voters. “I’m not making this up. He, the President of the United States, is spending time supergluing gold medallions to the mantel in the Oval Office,” Obama said in Atlanta late in the campaign. “He is literally doing arts and crafts while we are fighting a war and trying to deal with out-of-control prices.”

After being asked by a reporter about the criticism, Trump once again invoked a racist trope by suggesting that the nation’s first Black president is unclean.

“Obama doesn’t know about dirt and filth, and he doesn’t know about crack stone, and he doesn’t know about broken windows. He’s probably fine with them, but I’m not”, Trump said.

In the months leading up to the midterm elections, Donald Trump’s administration waged an aggressive effort to sow doubt about the results, intimidate voters and election officials, and signal they would use the power of the federal government to challenge the electoral process.

#198. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#197. OpenAI released a new cybersecurity model. Is that enough?

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Max Justicz, the chief technology officer and co-founder of the startup Charge Robotics, used an OpenAI model to find a severe vulnerability that — if exploited — could allow hackers to take control of servers running Ubuntu, a widely used open source Linux-based operating system.

Justicz, who has a hobbyist’s interest in cybersecurity and previously found a vulnerability in the same codebase, told TechCrunch that he found this issue using GPT 5.6 Sol, released in July, through the AI maker’s Daybreak Blue, a program that gives cybersecurity defenders access to powerful frontier models.

“I literally just aimed ChatGPT at the same body of code and said ‘I found this bug previously that was fixed several years ago, please find bugs as bad or worse,’ and it came back in two hours with this,” Justicz said. “It’s extremely convoluted and hard for a human to find, but it works!”

He reported the issue to the Debian Project, which maintains the software in question and issued a patch to fix the vulnerability. There has been no known evidence of exploitation. Still, the ease with which he found it suggests that we’ve only seen the tip of the iceberg when it comes to potential exploits that even novice hackers can utilize with the help of AI models.

“I can only imagine what one could do with more time and access to less neutered models. We should expect some extremely crazy bugs to be found and exploited over the next few months,” Justicz wrote in a social media thread discussing his finding. “This might be obvious to most folks here, but there is currently huge alpha in taking some code or binary someone has not audited yet and just aiming a powerful AI model at it.”

OpenAI appears to have noticed. Today, the AI giant announced a new model focused on cybersecurity, ChatGPT Cyber, would be made available to the highest tier of users in the cybersecurity program, Daybreak Red, which allows offensive cybersecurity researchers tools for vulnerability hunting and development. Yesterday, OpenAI said that the same model had been used to scan and prepare patches for vulnerabilities in the Linux kernel, finding 948 potential issues. OpenAI also found 81 “novel” vulnerabilities in Firefox, the web browser.

“Our goal is to dramatically expand Daybreak, bringing advanced security research to organizations of every size, independent researchers, and open-source maintainers,” the frontier lab said in a statement.

OpenAI has insisted that there is a narrow window of time for the people who safeguard digital infrastructure to take action before AI models become even better at hacking and easier to access. For cyber defenders, however, it can feel like the frontier labs have unleashed a mountain of new work that outstrips their resources. Justicz said that he personally spent about $5,000 on subscriptions and tokens to find several exploits.

Besides Daybreak’s model access, OpenAI has also announced a $1 billion initiative to support cybersecurity professionals in the program who work on critical infrastructure like the electrical grid or water systems.

The Daybreak program has its own critiques. Justicz notes that the frontier lab appears to have recently “nerfed” Daybreak Blue to make it less useful at finding vulnerabilities. On one hand, it may be good to limit access from potential bad actors — Justicz said he just uploaded a photo of his driver’s license to join the program. On the other, cybersecurity professionals say they have one hand tied if they can’t use frontier technology while bad actors can deploy open-weight models with abliterated guardrails.

Still, OpenAI says just “thousands” of cybersecurity professionals are participating in the Daybreak program, versus a far larger number of potential hackers.

It’s unclear if frontier labs are devoting enough of their resources to this issue. Eric Wallace, a researcher on OpenAI’s technical staff, said that “we are now allocating a significant amount of our frontier compute towards discovering and patching vulnerabilities in open source codebases, fully pro bono.”

But the Linux bug hunt cost $1.2 million, which is significantly less than the approximate $6 million token spend the company invested in finding a potential solution to a famous problem of fluid dynamics.

If there is a narrow window of time to patch the digital infrastructure the world depends on, more urgency may be required.
