#195. Henry Nowak’s killer has sentence extended by court of appeal

Vickrum Digwa’s ‘unduly lenient’ term for Southampton teenager’s murder increased after attorney general’s appeal

Henry Nowak’s killer has seen his sentence extended by the court of appeal after it ruled that his original sentence was “unduly lenient”.

Vickrum Digwa’s original sentence of life with a minimum term of 21 years was lengthened to a minimum of 24 years and 190 days after a successful appeal by the attorney general, Ellie Reeves.

Digwa, 23, stabbed Nowak several times in an attack in Southampton in December last year.

When police arrived at the murder scene, Digwa falsely claimed Nowak had racially abused him and knocked his turban off, causing officers to arrest and handcuff Nowak before they saw his fatal injuries.

Digwa filmed his victim as he lay dying and lied to emergency services that Nowak, 18, had racially abused him.

The incident led to riots in Southampton.

Lady Chief Justice Carr said the original sentencing judge, William Mousley KC, had materially understated two distinct dimensions of Digwa’s offending.

She said: “First, the circumstances in which a large and immediately available dagger was used repeatedly with an intention to kill an unarmed man, and secondly, the deliberate cruelty, additional suffering, and obstruction [of justice] which followed the stabbing.”

She added: “A minimum term of 21 years did not adequately reflect the overall seriousness of the murder. It was not only lenient, but unduly so.

“Therefore, we quash the minimum term of 21 years. We replace it with a minimum term of 25 years. From that term must be deducted the 175 days spent in custody on remand, producing an operative minimum term of 24 years and 190 days.”

In written submissions on behalf of Reeves, Jonathan Polnay KC said the sentence failed to take into account a series of “significant aggravating factors”, including Digwa’s “false allegation of racism against the deceased” and his “abuse” of the privilege afforded to Sikhs to carry a knife in a public place for religious reasons.

At Digwa’s sentencing hearing, Mousley said that on the night of the murder, Digwa had a small knife hidden from view called a kirpan, which is a strict requirement for Sikhs to carry at all times.

Digwa also had a “large dagger in a sheath” due to his membership of the Nihang, an order of Sikhs which has a tradition of holding a second knife.

Polnay told the court that the attempt to increase the sentence was “not about the lawful possession of a kirpan” and the laws allowing Sikhs to carry blades. “It is about what followed.” He said Digwa’s use of the dagger was “on at least one level, analogous to an abuse of trust”.

He continued: “When a person who has the benefit of that accommodation uses the article protected by law for repeated lethal violence, that is an aggravating factor of particular weight.”

Polnay also cited “the offender’s sustained attempts to pervert the course of justice” and “his procurement of his mother’s assistance in concealing evidence”.

Digwa’s mother, Kiran Kaur, 53, was jailed for three years for assisting an offender by taking the knife from where her son murdered Novak back to the family home.

Polnay said other aggravating factors included Digwa’s “degradation of the dying victim by filming him, and his conduct in misleading responders”.

His submission said: “The minimum term imposed failed adequately to reflect the combined weight of those features.”

In her ruling made alongside Mr Justice Nicklin and Mr Justice Wall, Carr said: “A person who knowingly carries a large knife or other bladed article in public, even if lawfully, and then uses it to kill an unarmed man after only a brief altercation, is highly culpable. His culpability is much closer to that of a person who takes a weapon into the street for offensive use than to that of someone who kills without a weapon, or someone who picks up a weapon of opportunity.”

On the false allegations of racism, Carr said: “It is unnecessary for us to determine whether the allegation of racial abuse was false, for even if that finding is left out of account, it does not affect our assessment of the appropriate minimum term.”

Digwa watched proceedings from HMP Frankland in County Durham, wearing a dark suit, shirt and tie.

Members of Nowak’s family attended the hearing at the Royal Courts of Justice, with one relative wiping their eyes as the ruling was handed down.

Imran Khan KC, for Digwa, said the sentence could be increased only in “exceptional, rather than borderline cases, and directed to gross error by the sentencing judge”. He asked: “Where is the gross error?”

Challenging the length of the sentence, Khan said that while Digwa’s lies to first responders “unquestionably affected the dignity with which Mr Nowak was treated”, his wounds were unsurvivable. “The false account therefore did not worsen the fatal injury or deprive Mr Nowak of the prospect of survival,” he said.

In response to Digwa’s appeal, Carr said: “Having reached the conclusion that the proper minimum term would be at least 25 years, we refuse Digwa leave to appeal against his sentence. It is unarguable that it is manifestly excessive.”

Reeves said: “The brutality and cruelty of this case horrified me, and I know that feeling is shared by the British public. I welcome the court of appeal’s decision to increase the minimum term of Digwa’s life sentence.”

→ Baca artikel asli

#194. Who is drug testing Russia's athletes, now that they have returned to international competition?

DW has found that three-quarters of Russia's weightlifting team had no independent out-of-competition testing before this year's world championships, despite the country's doping history.

Fresh questions have emerged over Russia's return to world sport after figures reviewed by DW showed that only four of the 16 Russian weightlifters competing at this year's world championships underwent out-of-competition testing by the International Testing Agency (ITA).

Two-time European champion Anastasiia Romanova, who previously served a two-year doping ban, was among those not tested by the ITA during the first nine months of 2026, according to its quarterly anti-doping reports.

By comparison, all 13 members of the North Korean team underwent at least one ITA test during the same period. North Korea, like Russia, has a history of doping violations in weightlifting.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The ITA, which runs weightlifting's anti-doping program, told DW that it tested all 16 Russian weightlifters "ahead of their participation in the event." DW understands those tests were conducted in Ningbo, China, where the championships are being held.

In a power sport like weightlifting, performance-enhancing drugs are most beneficial during training, when athletes build strength and muscle mass in the months before competition. Some drugs, such as anabolic steroids, may be harder to detect by the time the competition comes around.

Rob Koehler, head of the athlete-led movement Global Athlete, called the lack of out-of-competition testing "absurd."

"Out-of-competition testing is the backbone of anti-doping," Koehler told DW. "Everybody knows there's going to be testing at the event. But not everybody knows when they're going to be tested 365 days a year outside the events. It's concerning that the ITA doesn't see this as a red flag."

Marta Nawrocka, the ITA's spokesperson, said that Russian athletes were among the ten most-tested nationalities in weightlifting this year. She also defended the fact that only two Russian weightlifters feature in the sport's highest-priority testing pool, saying inclusion is based on performance and risk profiles.

"Russia's history of doping in weightlifting remains an important factor in the ITA's risk assessment and testing priorities," Nawrocka said, adding that "Russian weightlifters remain subject to substantial testing activity."

"You simply can't treat Russia like any other country," he said. "It has to have more scrutiny. It has to have more testing. If you don't do that, you're undermining athlete confidence in the system."

The International Weightlifting Federation did not respond to a request for comment.

Weightlifting was one of the sports most implicated in Russia's state-sponsored doping scandal, which came to light after the 2014 Sochi Winter Olympics. It ranked second only to athletics for the number of positive cases identified by Canadian lawyer Richard McLaren in his 2016 report commissioned by WADA, the global anti-doping watchdog.

The scandal led to dozens of Russian athletes being banned across numerous sports. RUSADA has remained suspended for most of the past decade.

As a result, its Moscow laboratory cannot analyze urine and blood samples, meaning samples collected by the agency must be sent abroad for analysis under what WADA described as a "strict chain of custody."

WADA said it continued to monitor RUSADA but had been unable to gain access to the country to review the agency's suspension because of the "ongoing geopolitical situation."

It added that RUSADA had "collected thousands of samples … including hundreds from weightlifters" so far this year.

"Given the history, WADA remains vigilant when it comes to Russia," James Fitzgerald, WADA's spokesperson, told DW. "We must ensure that no stone is left unturned when it comes to verifying that all the proper testing and other anti-doping activities are taking place."

Despite being suspended, RUSADA still has the power to investigate and prosecute anti-doping cases involving Russian athletes, which critics find difficult to reconcile.

"It's a compounding problem that WADA is still giving them a free pass when it can't even get into Russia to see what they're doing," said Koehler, WADA's former deputy director general.

"There's simply no oversight and no accountability. And quite frankly, I don't think there ever really has been."

Critics also argue that ongoing questions over RUSADA's leadership and independence suggest that little has been fixed in the decade since the McLaren report.

Even the International Olympic Committee (IOC), whose president, Kirsty Coventry, supports the full return of Russian athletes, has acknowledged a "lack of confidence" over RUSADA's governance.

Nawrocka said the IOC had "recently entrusted" the ITA with an expanded role within Russia in the run-up to the Los Angeles Olympics in 2028, but did not explain what the role would involve or when it might begin.

In April, investigative outlet The Insider reported that Veronika Loginova, the head of the agency, was in a long-term relationship with an alleged FSB officer. The FSB, Russia's domestic security service, played a central role in covering up positive drug tests at the Sochi Games.

Loginova did not respond to questions for this article but dismissed the allegations at the time as "all this nonsense," writing in an email that she believed "sport exists separate from politics."

WADA would only confirm to DW that its investigations unit was examining the allegations against Loginova and that the inquiry was ongoing.

→ Baca artikel asli

#193. Meta warns that election backlash against data centers could make AI expansion harder

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Meta president Dina Powell McCormick said that the Democratic wins in the midterm elections are going to make it a lot harder for companies to build data centers, adding that the industry has “not done as good a job as we needed to explain the benefits of these investments.”

Speaking at the WSJ Tech conference on Wednesday, the Meta exec pointed out that the backlash against data centers — which was very much on the ballot during this election season — was also tied to people’s feelings about AI technology overall.

“The visible sort of manifestation of AI is a data center, and it’s something that people can say, ‘I don’t want this in my community,’” she explained. “I think there’s an underlying bigger issue, which is how people feel about AI in general.”

Meta believes it could turn around the negative consumer sentiment around AI if it’s able to demonstrate the value, not only of the data centers coming to local communities, but also in the technology itself.

“The stakes of who builds the technology that cures cancer, that gives access to education, that is also a very fundamental difference … Mark [Zuckerberg, Meta CEO] is very ‘long human,’ and he’s betting on humans more than machines,” Powell McCormick explained. “And that’s what we mean when we say the future is for everyone. That people should not be left behind. But the way to ensure that is that this technology is democratized, that people have access to it in a very fair and transparent way,” she said.

The exec also touted Meta’s experience in building data centers, saying that the company has been doing so for 20 years, as it takes a lot of compute power to offer the products it does in the social networking space. Today, the company has data centers in 23 states, she said, and five of them, including one in Texas, are planned as projects of 1 gigawatt or more.

But Meta is now worried that it could lose some of its investments after politicians ran on anti-data-center platforms during the midterms.

“I think it’s going to be harder,” Powell McCormick said, describing what the future of building data centers looks like. “It has been hard, I should say, but I also think that it’s becoming clear what models work, and the states want those models that work.”

Specifically, communities only want data centers when it benefits them in some way, she explained, laying out the approach that Meta today uses to close its data center deals in local communities.

Successful projects see Meta paying for its own electricity, directing any tax surpluses as a result of the data centers toward teachers and first responders, keeping the labor force local, and getting bipartisan support for the project, among other things.

Going forward, she expects data center projects to come under more review and be weighed against whether the constituents actually want them.

Meta has pinned its hopes on its AI investments, particularly its consumer AI agent Muse scaling far beyond the 10 million users it has today, to become a business that one day serves billions. But even if that’s not the case, it believes that its investments in data centers will not be for nothing, as the compute itself will be valuable.

“We’re getting calls every day to lease that compute [we’ve built], and so one way or another, we’re seeing a path to monetization,” Powell McCormick said.

#192. Bank of England raises interest rates to 4%, the first increase for more than three years

Base rate increases from 3.75% and Bank governor signals another rise in near future to combat rising inflation

Analysis: Interest rate rise in midst of cost of living crisis will leave many incredulous

The Bank of England has raised interest rates for the first time in three years and warned of another increase to combat inflationary pressure unleashed by the Iran war.

Eight members of the Bank’s monetary policy committee (MPC) voted for the rise, which takes the base rate from 3.75% to 4%. The ninth member, Catherine Mann, preferred a more dramatic half-point increase.

The Middle East conflict has pushed oil prices persistently above $100 a barrel in recent months, as hopes have evaporated of a deal that could reopen the strait of Hormuz.

As a result, the Bank now expects UK inflation to rise to 4.9% in the middle of next year – up from 3.2% in its last economic forecast in July. Inflation hit 3.5% in September, as measured on the consumer prices index.

The decision, which will hit mortgage borrowers, comes a week after John Healey used his first budget to promise “breathing space” for hard-pressed consumers. Echoing the Office for Budget Responsibility’s gloomy forecasts for living standards, the MPC expects real post-tax income to fall this year and next.

The Bank’s governor, Andrew Bailey, raised the prospect of another rate rise in the near future – with a resolution to the Iran conflict the key factor that will influence the decision.

“Without news which softens the risks to inflationary pressures, I can see the argument for a further increase in rates and thereafter taking a more conditional approach,” he said, according to the MPC minutes.

However, he played down the likelihood of a prolonged series of increases, as markets have been predicting. “Today’s increase shouldn’t be seen as the first in an inevitable long sequence in hikes. But unless the conflict in the Middle East is resolved, neither does it seem likely to be the last increase,” he said.

The Bank’s next interest rate decision will be announced on 17 December, and some analysts suggested the MPC could press ahead with a pre-Christmas hike.

Andrew Goodwin, the chief economist at consultancy Oxford Economics, said: “The degree of inflationary pressure already in the system means another hike in December or February is likely.”

Explaining its decision to raise borrowing costs, the MPC highlighted the pass-through from elevated oil prices to business and consumer costs across the economy. It expects the Ofgem energy cap to rise from the equivalent of £1,723 a year for an average household in Great Britain to £2,150 in January and to increase again in April. Food price inflation could hit 4.75% by mid-2027, it warned.

Businesses have so far reported in the Bank’s surveys that they have been able to absorb soaring oil and gas prices – by hedging their energy costs, for example – but the MPC believes they will soon have to start passing them on to consumers in higher prices.

It is also concerned about workers seeking to offset the impact of the higher cost of living by asking for bumper pay rises.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Oil prices have fallen sharply, to below $100 a barrel, after the Democrats’ victories in the US Congress, which some investors have interpreted as potentially constraining Donald Trump’s capacity to prolong the conflict in Iran. Trump’s vice-president, JD Vance, has blamed the war, and its impact on inflation, for the rout at the polls.

Speaking at the Bank’s press conference, Bailey welcomed the sharp fall in energy prices in the last 48 hours but said it would need to be sustained to change his view about the need for future rate rises. “This is positive news, but there is already a significant amount of inflationary pressure in the pipeline,” he said, adding: “What is the best thing that can happen? It is to resolve the conflict in the Middle East.”

While the decision to increase rates was supported by all MPC members, the minutes of the meeting reveal stark divisions over how much further they may need to go to bring inflation under control. The dovish Alan Taylor suggested this should be a “risk-management ‘one-for-now’ insurance hike”, while Mann argued that an immediate half-point rise was necessary to restore the Bank’s inflation-fighting credibility.

Healey’s budget included measures aimed at cushioning the impact of higher energy prices – including extending the fuel duty freeze and the VAT cut on electricity bills. The MPC said the package would shave a quarter percentage point off inflation in mid-2027.

Responding to the interest rate increase, Healey said: “The Iran conflict is reaching deep into the pockets of families and businesses here at home.

“In my budget I acted to give some breathing space to families and businesses. Twenty million households will get a £100 energy payment, I extended the tax cut on electricity bills and froze fuel duty. I also set out the course ahead to get costs down for good.”

But the shadow chancellor, Andrew Griffith, called the rate rise “another headwind for hardworking families to deal with”.

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

#188. Serve Robotics launches new business to become the AWS for robots

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Serve Robotics, a maker of self-driving sidewalk delivery robots, is expanding beyond its autonomous delivery operations in a bid to become a technology supplier to other companies building robots.

The company, which spun out of Uber’s acquisition of Postmates in 2021 and went public in 2024, said Thursday it will launch a new business line called Physical AI Services.

Specifically, Serve Robotics will offer customers its connectivity and data tools. Bedrock Robotics, a startup developing self-driving systems for construction equipment, is a customer, according to Serve. It’s also working with T-Mobile to bring AI computing power closer to robots, Serve announced alongside its third-quarter earnings on Thursday.

“We are solving the problem of robots in human spaces,” Serve Robotics CEO and co-founder Ali Kashani told TechCrunch.

Serve Robotics already makes two kinds of robots, the cute googly-eyed sidewalk delivery robot and one designed for hospitals that it got through its acquisition of Diligent Robotics.

Those businesses are not going away, Kashani emphasized in a recent interview. Instead, he said there is opportunity to sell the technology it developed for its own robots to other companies developing robots for farms, warehouses, and other uncontrolled environments.

“I kind of think of it as the AWS moment where we are taking the technology we’ve built for us, and we’re opening it up for more people to build on,” he said, adding this wouldn’t have been possible without developing its own robot fleet.

The expansion comes as Serve’s sidewalk delivery business expands, but is shifting away from Uber and toward other partners, including DoorDash and Wonder.

Its new Physical AI Services business line will offer three products. Serve Link helps connect robots in the field to AI systems running in data centers, which would allow companies to potentially tap into more powerful AI models without having to put all that computing power onboard.

“Any robotic application generally needs to connect the robots to some offline intelligence, whether it’s to collect data or do exception handling or whatever,” said Kashani. “It’s the connectivity piece that has to be really high performance. You need to be able to manage really unreliable networks and still reliably connect, and that’s what we solve.”

It’s also launching a data curator tool that helps companies find edge cases in the reams of real-world data a robot collects, which can then be fed back into a training model to improve performance. Its third product is a real-world, foundation-model data catalog built from Serve’s own sidewalk robots that can teach AI models how to understand complex human environments.

Kashani said he’s been thinking about this idea — a playbook he learned from Amazon — for years.

“When the technology is so nascent, you have got to build the end-to-end application because that’s the only way you know what to build, but then over time, as the technology gets more advanced, a lot more people are going to be wanting to build similar things,” he said. “And what you want to do is to open your technology so others build with you and on your platform.”

#186. EU court says CBD foods need approval for sale before they can be certified organic

The EU's top court says CBD foods cannot be certified organic without approval for sale. The ruling concerns a cannabis compound whose use in food requires authorization, including before organic certification.

The European Union's top court ruled Thursday that foods containing cannabidiol, or CBD, cannot receive organic certification without first being authorized for sale in the bloc.

The court said an organic label could mislead shoppers into thinking CBD foods had been approved for sale.

CBD is a compound found in cannabis, commonly extracted from hemp — cannabis varieties with low levels of THC. Unlike THC, CBD does not produce a high.

The compound is widely marketed in oils, capsules and gummies as a wellness ingredient, often with claims about sleep, stress or pain relief.

The European Commission classifies CBD as a "novel food" — a food not consumed to a significant degree in the EU before May 15, 1997. Such foods require a safety assessment and authorization before they can legally be marketed.

The court noted that CBD has not yet received that authorization, although it did not say that it was unsafe.

CBD is also found in medical marijuana, but the ruling concerns its use in food rather than medicines.

The court said meeting organic production standards did not remove the need for market authorization.

"Such certification contributes to the promotion of those products and conveys, in the eyes of consumers, a positive image of quality and approval," the court said.

The restriction also covers products intended for export outside the EU.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The ruling concerns CBD-based or CBD-enriched foods that lack EU market authorization, rather than all cannabis-derived foods.

Some cannabis-derived foods, such as hemp seeds and ordinary hemp seed oil, can legally be sold and certified organic if they meet the relevant standards.

France's organic farming regulator, INAO, told certification bodies that CBD-based products and CBD-enriched foods could not be certified organic. It also barred CBD as an ingredient in certified organic foods, while exempting hemp oil not enriched with CBD.

A hemp industry association challenged those restrictions before France's Council of State, which referred the case to the EU court.

The French court must now resolve the dispute in line with the EU court's interpretation.

#185. Cancer diagnoses in England hit record high of more than 1,000 people a day

While NHS is identifying more cases, it is failing to meet timescale targets on diagnosis and treatment, experts say

Cancer diagnoses in England have hit record highs, with more than 1,000 people a day diagnosed, NHS figures show.

But experts warned that although the health service was identifying more cases, it was still failing to meet timescale targets on diagnosis and treatment.

According to the latest cancer registration statistics, more than 373,000 people were diagnosed with cancer in England in 2024, the highest figure on record. On average, NHS England diagnosed 1,021 people a day with cancer in 2024, up from 972 a day in 2023.

Prostate, breast, lung and bowel cancers accounted for half of all new diagnoses.

The figures showed improvements in stage at diagnosis: 56.7% of first new cancers were diagnosed at stage 1 and 2 in 2024 compared with 55.8% in 2023.

Prof Peter Johnson, the NHS national clinical director for cancer, said the figures showed the health service was “moving in the right direction” and were testimony to NHS staff’s hard work.

He said: “NHS teams across the country are taking tests and checks closer to the people who need them, and with new treatments being developed all the time, we will continue to do all we can to get people seen and treated for cancer as early as possible.”

Experts urged caution, however, given the NHS is failing to meet its timescale targets. The latest monthly NHS figures show that 78.6% of patients in England urgently referred for suspected cancer were diagnosed or had cancer ruled out within 28 days in August – below the target of 80%.

The health service is lagging even further behind on treatment. The proportion of patients who began treatment for cancer within 62 days of an urgent referral or consultant upgrade was 71.9%, well below the target of 85%.

Dr Ian Walker, the executive director of policy at Cancer Research UK, said: “While NHS staff are diagnosing more people with cancer every day, too many are still being caught at a late stage.”

The health service needed additional support to reduce the number of cancers diagnosed at a late stage and treat patients on time, he added.

Walker said: “The UK government pledged to improve early diagnosis, cancer waiting times and access to treatment in its national cancer plan for England. It now needs to keep its foot on the pedal to ensure cancer patients aren’t let down. The situation won’t change without focused effort from the government, clear accountability, and a guarantee that the NHS will get the specialist staff and equipment it needs.”

Dr Hilary Williams, the clinical vice-president at the Royal College of Physicians and a consultant in medical oncology, said: “More than 1,000 cancer diagnoses every day is a stark reminder of the growing demand on NHS cancer services.

“An ageing population is an important factor in the increase in diagnoses, but we must also recognise the role of obesity, which is a major and often overlooked risk factor for cancer.” She added that alongside preventing cancer, the NHS needed to continue improving early detection, including through the expansion of targeted lung cancer screening.

Williams said: “As more people are diagnosed and live longer with cancer, we must ensure they can access the specialist care and support they need.”

Steven McIntosh, the chief partnerships officer at Macmillan Cancer Support, said the figures also masked persistent health inequalities. “We know that who you are and where you live can still have a significant impact on how likely you are to get cancer, as well as how quickly you are diagnosed and your chances of survival,” he said.

Rachael Franklin, the deputy chief executive of Breast Cancer Now, said with diagnoses at an all-time high and overall incidence increasing, “breast cancer remains the biggest killer of working-age women in the UK, with one in seven women diagnosed in their lifetime”.

“That’s why it’s so important that people of all ages are breast aware and get anything new or unusual checked by a GP as soon as possible. We also urge all eligible women to attend their screening appointment when invited,” she said.

#184. Airbnb says it’s launching an AI travel agent in the first half of 2027

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Airbnb has teased its plans to build its own AI travel agent before, but on Thursday the company announced that the new AI feature will launch in the first half of 2027. In the company’s Q3 shareholder letter on Thursday, Airbnb also provided more detail about how the agent will work, what it will actually do, and how it’s different from a typical AI chatbot.

In prior interviews, CEO Brian Chesky sketched out the travel company’s approach to AI, describing a plan to build an AI agent that has a richer interface than a conventional chatbot. He also expressed his opinions about how chatbots aren’t the right interface for travel, noting that people take pleasure in the planning process and in anticipating a trip. Any agent that it built, then, shouldn’t take away from the inspiration and the browsing, he told TechCrunch in an interview last month.

Chesky today reiterated that belief, telling analysts on the earnings call that people won’t use one AI agent for everything.

“Today’s AI agents like Muse and Instinct are good at simple routine tasks like making a restaurant reservation or scheduling an appointment. But planning a trip is different,” he said. “Travel is aspirational. Discovering where to go and where to stay is part of the experience. In fact, 80% of travelers say they enjoy spending time researching trips. People want to look at photos. They want to compare homes. They want to make decisions with people they’re traveling with,” he continued. “And I don’t think the general-purpose agents are the best to do that. So that is why we are building our own AI agent,” Chesky said.

Airbnb described its agent as having a personalized interface that’s designed specifically for travel, where it will “build an interface” around a user’s question instead of simply responding as a chatbot would.

“What we are doing is developing a custom-designed AI-native experience that responds to what guests are looking for,” Chesky said. “So instead of relying on traditional chat alone, which is very text-heavy—which, again, is definitely not the way you should plan travel, our agent draws from a library of hundreds of travel-specific interface components—maps, photos, comparisons, calendars, itineraries—and composes them in real time to match what they’re looking for.”

The exec said the agent will feel “a little bit like generative UI” as it’s designed to offer up “the right interface at the right time” for someone’s specific travel needs.

In its shareholder letter, the company touted that its nearly 20 years of experience in bringing hosts and guests together is informing how the agent is built, as it incorporates insights about how people both book and host on its platform. The agent will also be able to help travelers beyond the booking itself.

“Our agent will be able to help guests throughout their trip—from changing a reservation or resolving a payment issue to getting help with refunds, rebooking, or 24-hour safety support. These are things an outside agent can’t easily do on its own,” the company wrote.

Over time, Airbnb expects the agent will be able to help travelers with other aspects of their trip, including not only homes and hotels, but also services and experiences. Services and experiences are two areas that have become a bigger focus for the company following its redesign last year.

While the company previously said to expect its AI agent sometime in the next year, it’s now more specifically committing to roll out the new technology to consumers in the first half of 2027.

The news of the agent’s release was delivered alongside strong third-quarter earnings, with revenue growing 19% to $4.87 billion, topping Wall Street estimates. It also raised its 2026 revenue growth forecast, which it now predicts will be in the high teens, instead of the mid-teens.

“Airbnb is back in growth mode,” Chesky told analysts on the Q3 earnings call on Thursday. “It’s rare for a marketplace of our size to reaccelerate growth, but that’s exactly what we’re doing. Q3 was our fastest revenue growth in more than three years,” he added.

#183. Police investigating two further rape allegations against Russell Brand

Detective urges other potential victims to come forward and says: ‘Our work’s not done’

The lead detective in the case against Russell Brand has urged other potential victims of the comedian to come forward after it emerged he is under investigation for two further rape allegations and faces a civil claim in the UK.

Det Supt Andy Furphy, the Metropolitan police’s strategic lead in the investigation, described Brand, 51, as “a predator” whose behaviour escalated as his fame grew.

On Thursday, Brand was found guilty of raping a fan and sexually assaulting three women over five years at the height of his career. He was cleared of two further counts of rape and one count of attempted indecent assault after a four-week trial at Southwark crown court.

It can now be revealed that Brand is under investigation for two further rape allegations and that the Met has submitted files to the Crown Prosecution Service.

Brand has also been accused of sexually assaulting a woman at his mansion in Los Angeles in 2008, in a civil claim filed at the high court in London.

The Met’s investigation into Brand, named Operation Actinolite, involved tracing and speaking to more than 100 witnesses, reviewing more than 1,400 documents and taking more than 340 statements, including from Brand’s former employers, colleagues and associates.

Speaking after Brand’s conviction, Furphy said the police investigation was continuing and he believed there could be more victims.

“If you get a conviction then it tends to encourage other people to come forward because they see the courage and the bravery and the resilience that these women have had to go through this criminal justice process, and they feel their own strength as a result of that. They want to come forward and have their story told,” he told the Guardian.

“So, yes, there may be more victims of Russell Brand out there, but for now our focus remains on the women that came forward.”

The Met said Brand had been interviewed under caution in connection with two further rape allegations made by two women. “Files have been submitted to the Crown Prosecution Service in relation to both complainants. Detectives remain in close contact with prosecutors as inquiries continue and further investigative work is carried out,” a spokesperson said.

Pre-trial legal documents suggested there were outstanding charges against Brand in relation to a woman who made allegations about him in a Channel 4 Dispatches documentary in 2023. She accused Brand of grooming her and sending a BBC car to bring her to him from the gates of her school when she was a 16-year-old pupil.

Furphy refused to comment on any outstanding inquiries but appealed for women to come forward if they had anything to report.

“We have a live investigation. Our work’s not done,” Furphy said. “What I would say, is if you’ve been affected by the case, if Russell Brand has committed a crime against you, rape or serious sexual violence or a sex assault, whatever it might be, and you haven’t spoken to police yet, then come forward. We’ve got specially trained staff, specially trained officers. We’ll support you and listen to you.”

It is understood a couple of other women made allegations against Brand during the Met’s investigation but they did not result in charges.

Furphy said Brand had abused his celebrity status to assault women who then feared they would not be believed.

“Russell Brand is a predator,” Furphy said. “He preys on women and commits serious sexual offences against them. He’s in a position of power, a celebrity, he’s wealthy.

“If you look at the offending from the first date up until when the final offences were committed, you can see an escalation in his behaviour. The more famous he gets, the more emboldened he gets, less likely to be challenged, less likely to be held accountable. He’s been held accountable now.”

He praised the “really courageous, brave and resilient women” who gave evidence against him while facing the intense scrutiny of Brand’s barrister, Ian Winter KC. He described the cross-examination as “deeply uncomfortable”.

“Rape and serious sexual offences is a deeply personal thing, people are recounting some of the worst things that have ever happened to them,” he said. “We can perceive that line of questioning may come, but I’ve got to be honest with you, it was deeply uncomfortable at times.”

Brand gave interviews to Tucker Carlson, Megyn Kelly and Piers Morgan before the trial and continued podcasting throughout, which the detective said had taken a toll on the women.

“It’d be fair to say that what he did to them has had a very long-lasting effect on them, in the way they trust people … it’s been devastating to many of them,” Furphy said.

“Then a challenging aspect of this [case] was the constant public commentary around it, and some of that was coming from him. And as you can imagine in a case like this, as an investigation team, we’ve got to review everything – DVDs, books, podcasts, stuff that’s appearing online.

“So, for me, he drove right up to the line of what was acceptable in a live criminal trial and stopped just short. But that doesn’t mean we weren’t prepared to take action if it started to cross over that line, and the women who’ve put their trust in us knew that.”

Furphy said the case may be a “watershed moment” in inspiring a wave of other victims of sexual offences to come forward. “I think if you look back to that culture during that period of time, that kind of lad culture, there may be other victims that come forward regarding other people.”

Brand is already facing civil claims in Britain and the US. In court papers seen by the Guardian, a woman who has begun civil proceedings in London has claimed Brand forcibly removed her dress, shoes and underwear, pushed her on to his bed and attempted “to perform oral sex on her against her will”.

She said she eventually managed to break free from his grasp but was left “terrified and shaking violently” as she ran from the house. She said she later suffered from PTSD symptoms. Brand denies her claims, which he described via his lawyers as “fundamentally dishonest”.

The case was lodged last year but was stayed until the proceedings in the criminal case against Brand concluded this week. A claim form filed in February 2025 suggests the woman is seeking damages of up to £100,000.

The woman said she felt “traumatised by the rage Brand displayed when she refused to submit to him sexually” and feared her safety would be at risk if she pursued a criminal complaint.

However, she decided to report her allegation to the Met in 2025. She said she was “fortified” by other women who had come forward to make allegations against Brand.

The woman’s case was not included in the trial. The Guardian understands this was largely because the offence was alleged to have occurred in the US, meaning the English criminal courts did not have jurisdiction to prosecute.

A separate civil claim was filed in the US in 2023 over allegations Brand sexually assaulted a woman in 2010 while filming the movie Arthur.

None of the victims in the criminal case have submitted compensation claims.

#181. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

Iran blames Israel for the killing of second IRGC officer, vows to respond

A second Iranian Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) officer died following an attack in Syria on Friday, according to Iranian state media on Sunday. What’s the difference between Persians and Arabs?Comedian @MazJobrani shares with me his thoughts on touring the Middle East, his support for Iran’s protests, and his latest comedy special: pic.twitter.com/UvYwVgJIlw

An Iranian state news outlet is gloating at what it sees as the demise of the US dollar.

IRNA recreated a popular meme to mark China and Brazil’s decision to reportedly ditch the US dollar as an intermediary in trade, citing the Chinese state news outlet, China Daily. It shows two men representing China and Brazil posing in front of a grave labelled “USD.”

The meme was pinned to the top of IRNA’s Twitter page, and was met with laughter and ridicule. “Dream on,” said another user, pointing to the dollar’s use as the main reserve currency around the world.

China Daily said that the agreement was part of “the rising global use of the Chinese renminbi.” It would reportedly enable China and Brazil to conduct trade and financial transactions using local currencies instead of the dollar.

#180. Iran signals satisfaction, skepticism after US midterm vote

While the Iranian regime says the US midterms will not change Washington's policy toward Tehran, some officials and commentators see the resounding Democratic victory as blowback against the Iran war.

Iran's leadership has downplayed the significance of Republican setbacks in the US midterm elections, insisting that the Democratic takeover of Congress is unlikely to alter Washington's policy toward the Islamic Republic.

"If there are still those who believe that America can be persuaded to abandon its hostility toward the Iranian nation through concessions or partisan changes in the US political system, they are gravely mistaken," Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei said in a statement after the vote.

Khamenei also warned that Tehran would respond more forcefully to any future US military action.

The elections took place after months of military confrontation between Tehran and Washington, which disrupted global energy markets and raised fears of a broader regional conflict.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Mohammad Sadegh Javadi Hesar, Iran-based journalist and member of the reformist party Etemad-e Melli, said the election outcome was unlikely to affect Iran's calculations.

"Iran will not change its position simply because Republicans have lost their majority," he told DW.

According to Javadi Hesar, decisions about ending the conflict will ultimately be made in Washington. Tehran, in turn, is expected to continue insisting on its previous demands for peace, such as the end of all hostilities, including in Lebanon, and the lifting of US naval blockade.

Others in Iran interpreted the results more positively.

Parliament Speaker Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf argued that American voters had delivered their verdict on the Iranian conflict.

"The Americans said no to war," Ghalibaf said on X, while acknowledging that rising cost of living also contributed to Donald Trump's party losing the election.

Ghalibaf also mocked US Vice President JD Vance who had acknowledged that the Iran war was the reason the Republicans lost.

"Skip the TED talk. Why not say this to your boss?" Ghalibaf wrote on X.

Mohsen Rezaei, secretary of Iran's Supreme National Security Council, said Iran not only challenged the United States on the battlefield and in the Strait of Hormuz but had also dealt it a political setback in Washington.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Several Iranian media outlets echoed that assessment. The news website Etelaat Online sarcastically suggested that the long-discussed "regime change" had ultimately taken place not in Iran, but in the United States.

Some commentators drew historical parallels to another period of US-Iran tension.

The state-affiliated Mehr News Agency compared Trump's political troubles to those of former US President Jimmy Carter, recalling the failed 1980 American military operation to rescue hostages held in Tehran.

At the time, the US deployed eight military helicopters to rendezvous with six transport planes in the Iranian desert before moving on Tehran to free the hostages. However, the aircraft encountered a sand storm and a deadly crash between a helicopter and a C-130 transport plane, killing eight Americans.

The agency argued that the 1980 episode demonstrated the limits of US military power in resolving disputes with Iran. Carter went on to lose the presidential election to Ronald Reagan later that year.

Iran released the hostages in January 1981, just minutes after Reagan was sworn in for his first presidential term.

Hardline media, however, dismissed the election's broader significance.

Tasnim News Agency, which is close to Iran's Revolutionary Guards, argued that there is little meaningful difference between Republicans and Democrats when it comes to Iran policy. According to the agency, both parties pursue similar goals toward the Islamic Republic despite differences in rhetoric.

Using a Persian proverb, Tasnim summed up its view bluntly: "The yellow dog is the brother of the jackal," suggesting that neither side should be expected to act differently toward Iran.

Veteran Iranian diplomat Hossein Alizadeh says the actual midterm fallout is difficult to predict, but notes US politics sends ripples out across the entire world.

"The USA is a superpower and a hegemonic power. Consequently, developments in that country are of significance to the entire world — from Mexico and Canada, through the European Union, to China, and, of course, the Middle East," he told DW.

Alizadeh is an expert in international relations who served in the diplomatic corps of the Islamic Republic for 22 years, before resigning in 2010 in protest of the 2009 Iranian post-election crackdown.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The Finland-based analyst considers another large-scale military escalation by the USA to be unlikely. While the Democrats are also opponents of the Islamic Republic, "unlike the Trump administration, they opted for a diplomatic agreement with Tehran. The 2015 nuclear deal placed Iranian nuclear activities under the oversight of the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA)," says Alizadeh.

He points out that the Democrats and the US allies were not in favor or attacking Iran.

"The negative repercussions of such a war have already become clear," he told DW.

#178. British Ebola patient evacuated from DR Congo arrives at London hospital

Doctor who was helping response to outbreak in Africa taken to specialist isolation unit at Royal Free hospital

A British doctor who tested positive for Ebola while working in the Democratic Republic of the Congo has returned to the UK for specialist treatment.

The patient, whose identity is unknown, was transported home on a medical evacuation flight, the Department of Health and Social Care said on Thursday. They are now being cared for in a specialised high-level isolation unit at the Royal Free hospital in Hampstead, north London.

Dr Renu Bindra, the deputy director for all-hazards public health response at the UK Health Security Agency, said: “UKHSA and the NHS have well-established and robust infection prevention and control protocols to limit the risk of onward transmission, and the risk to the wider public remains low.”

A humanitarian organisation sent the doctor to DR Congo to help in the response to the outbreak, which is the second largest on record. It is the first case involving a British national contracting Ebola in the current outbreak.

The Royal Free's isolation unit has strict protocols in place to prevent the patient coming into contact with any members of the public, and all staff treating the patient are equipped with the necessary personal protective equipment alongside other measures to prevent transmission.

Ebola is a highly contagious, severe and often fatal illness spread through direct contact with body fluids such as the blood or vomit of infected people, or dead bodies.

The current outbreak, declared in May by DR Congo’s health ministry, has surpassed 10,000 cases and 5,000 deaths. It has spread to Kenya and Uganda in east Africa, and last month Nigeria confirmed a case in its capital, Abuja.

People with the disease tend to fall ill two to 21 days after contact with an orthoebolavirus. Initial symptoms include fever, aches, pains, and fatigue, but can then progress to diarrhoea, vomiting and unexplained bleeding.

Get the day’s headlines and highlights emailed direct to you every morning

There have only been four confirmed cases of the virus in the UK previously. A case in 1976 was from a laboratory needle incident, and the three others involved health workers who were responding to an outbreak in West Africa between 2014 and 2015.

#177. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#174. Myanmar’s Aung San Suu Kyi ‘sounds strong’ but is isolated, son says

Kim Aris tells the Guardian chance to speak with detained Nobel laureate was ‘public relations’ move from military leaders

Myanmar’s former democratic leader Aung San Suu Kyi appears to be in good physical and mental health but is living in near-complete isolation, her son has said after the first call with his 81-year-old mother in more than five years.

“She still sounds certainly all there mentally, and physically she sounds strong,” Kim Aris said. “I don’t think she has anybody outside of the military or the police around her, so essentially she’s been held in solitary confinement from what I gather.”

After being deposed in a 2021 military coup, the Nobel Peace prize laureate has been moved to an unspecified location in the capital. “I’m still not entirely sure where she is,” said Aris. “I think it’s a residence in Naypyidaw, which is fenced off and it has a scrubby bit of land around it.”

Aris, who has lived in the UK most of his life and whose late father was a British historian, was allowed to speak to his mother on Thursday at the Myanmar embassy in London. While he welcomed the chance to talk, he said the invitation was part of a “public relations” campaign by the country’s military leaders.

The former junta chief Min Aung Hlaing has sought to rebuild international diplomatic ties, and in April switched his military fatigues for civilian clothes after elections that were widely condemned as a sham.

Activists have long demanded “proof of life” for Aung San Suu Kyi, as well as scores of other political prisoners. In August, she was finally photographed during a Red Cross visit. Last week, a government spokesperson published images of her smiling in a wood-panelled room as officials delivered two gardening books given to her as gifts by Britain’s King Charles. A paper calendar on the wall behind her showed the date.

Aris said these photo-ops were well-known stunts that the regime had used before to pretend his mother, a national icon, was being treated fairly. The daughter of the assassinated national independence hero Gen Aung San has spent much of her life since the late 1980s in and out of house arrest for leading pro-democracy movements.

“She remains completely shut off from the rest of the world. I think she’s unaware of what’s happening outside of where she is because she’s not allowed any newspapers or radio or television,” Aris said. “She’s been locked up for five years on trumped-up, politically motivated charges and she should be free.”

Ruled for decades by generals and suffering from western sanctions, Myanmar experienced a brief democratic moment after Aung San Suu Kyi was allowed to run in a 2015 election that her party overwhelmingly won. She took up the role of “state counsellor” under a civilian-military power-sharing agreement.

Her international reputation was soon tarnished after the 2016-2017 mass killings and expulsions of Rohingya Muslims. Aung San Suu Kyi flew to the international court of justice in The Hague, where she said she acknowledged military units might have used “disproportionate force”, but defended the government against accusations of genocide.

Aris said that his mother was never “silent” on the Rohingya crisis and what she said on the issue “could fill volumes”. Under her leadership, Myanmar launched a domestic inquiry and appointed the former UN chief Kofi Annan to head a commission.

He added that as state counsellor, Aung San Suu Kyi was “always walking on a tightrope”, knowing the military could strip her of her fragile power at any moment.

“The very hard-won nascent democracy that she was trying to lead was holding on to things by a thread,” he said. “The military could have taken over at any point, and they were doing their best to make my mother’s situation a lot more difficult and to undermine her position. And I think in that respect they did a pretty good job.”

The 2021 power grab by the military triggered a civil war that has killed more than 100,000 people, according to conflict monitors.

Aris said the level of public global support for his mother had “dwindled incredibly” since the Rohingya crisis and that the whole country was now suffering from a lack of international attention. While many governments have called for Aung San Suu Kyi’s release, she is not treated as the beloved international figure that she once was.

“Never mind my mother, the fact that people ignore what’s happening in Burma shouldn’t be down to what was being done to the Rohingya,” he said, using the country’s former name. “And that seems to be the case sometimes, that people say: ‘Oh, well, look how the Rohingya were treated. We can ignore what’s happening in Burma.’”

#172. British comedian and actor Russell Brand found guilty of rape

Prosecutors labeled Brand a sexual predator. He was charged in sexual assault cases spanning from 1999 to 2009.

UK comedian Russell Brand was found guilty on Thursday of one rape and three sexual assault charges. The sentences was handed by a jury in London's Southwark Crown Court.

Brand was cleared of two rapes and one count of attempted indecent assault.

Prosecutors had labeled Brand a ruthless sexual predator and brought up charges against in cases spanning from 1999 to 2009, the years where he rose in prominence from stand-up comedy in small venues to ‌becoming a television presenter and Hollywood film star.

Judge Joel Bennathan on Thursday said Brand faced a "significant" prison term, with sentencing due Monday next week.

Investigative journalists from The Sunday Times and Channel 4 Dispatches published accusations in September 2023, from women who said they had been raped, sexual abused, and degraded by Brand.

The revelations encouraged four women to come forward to criminally accuse him, while another two women came forward once Brand had been charged.

Evidence at the trial revealed Brand to be a man who abused his fame to obtain sex while treating women as objects, with his entourage and showbiz bosses accused of "normalizing" sexual misconduct and looking the other way on complaints against him.

The victims accused Brand in court of forcing them into sexual activity, sometimes within seconds of them first meeting.

Brand was known for risqué comedy stand-up routines and he rose to prominence in the early 2000s, hosting television programs and his own BBC radio show.

He also appeared in Hollywood films including "Forgetting Sarah Marshall" and "Get Him to the Greek."

Last year, Brand pleaded not guilty to the original charges and saying he was "grateful" for the "opportunity" to defend himself ahead of the trial.

"I was a drug addict, a sex addict and an imbecile, but what I never was was a rapist," he said about the charges. "I've never engaged in non-consensual activity."

Brand said the criminal case against him had a devastating impact on his life and family.

"My entire life has been consumed by these events, these allegations, these accusations," Brand said.

He moved away from mainstream media in recent years and relocated to Florida, where he has built a large online following with videos mixing wellness, conspiracy theories and discussions of his religious devotion.

#167. Paraguay: President Pena resigns amid bank crisis

Paraguay's President Santiago Pena has resigned amid a crisis over his ties to collapsed lender Ueno. Millions of customers' accounts are frozen, with public pension and health care funds also tied up.

Paraguayan President Santiago Pena resigned Thursday under mounting pressure over the collapse of digital lender Ueno, as lawmakers debated his removal from office.

The bank's accounts, also used to process pension payments, are frozen, leaving millions of customers unable to access their money. Ueno also held hundreds of millions of dollars in public funds.

Pena's resignation followed the withdrawal of support by Congress President Basilio Nunez and ruling conservative Colorado Party leader Horacio Cartes.

Thirty-five opposition lawmakers and ruling-party dissidents had submitted impeachment articles in the 80-seat Chamber of Deputies, accusing Pena of conflicts of interest, misuse of pension funds and institutional cover-ups linked to Ueno.

Pena sold his shares in Ueno Holding, whose businesses include the bank, in April 2025. Lawmakers also cited two loans he received from Ueno as part of allegations of improper financial gain.

They alleged improper presidential interference in the Social Security Institute, known as IPS, and preferential treatment for Ueno. IPS manages pension and health care funds for most Paraguayan workers and held $355 million at the bank — about 9% of its investments, according to financial manager Gladys Vera.

The motion also questioned Pena's 2023 appointment of central bank chief Carlos Carvallo, who previously chaired Financiera Ueno. According to the document, the central bank approved the company's conversion into a bank just 83 days after his appointment.

Ueno served about 2.94 million customers, roughly 60% of Paraguay's adult population.

As of last month, the bank said it held roughly $800 million in public-sector deposits — about a quarter of its total deposits — including money from the operators of Paraguay's giant hydroelectric dams.

Paraguay's central bank ordered a resolution or closure process Tuesday after finding that Ueno's solvency ratio had fallen below half the required minimum.

Ueno's app, card payments and transfers stopped working, while branches closed, leaving customers queuing outside unable to withdraw their savings.

Hundreds of employees of Ueno and other companies in its parent conglomerate protested outside the Labor Ministry Thursday, demanding unpaid wages and severance.

Retirees, depositors, students and workers had marched through Asuncion a day earlier, demanding Pena's impeachment and holding signs saying "Give us back our money."

Authorities detained eight senior executives, including founder Miguel Vazquez and bank president Juan Manuel Gustale. Prosecutors also charged 27 bank officials and business associates with offenses including breach of trust and attempted fraud.

Lawyers for several detainees said they needed time to review the allegations before commenting.

Pena had sought to reassure customers in a video message Wednesday, pledging legislation "that will return to every saver what they are entitled to as quickly as possible."

The crisis also forced him to cancel his attendance at the Ibero-American summit in Madrid.

Pena, a conservative economist, took office in August 2023 for a five-year term.
