#112. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#108. Bomb threats disrupt at least 25 flights across Europe, raising hybrid war fears

Planes forced into emergency landings or diversions two days after hoax alerts affected air travel

Why have more than 30 European flights been hit by bomb threats this week?

Hoax bomb threats have caused at least 25 flights across Europe to be disrupted, with some forced to make emergency landings, as questions mount over an apparently coordinated effort to cause chaos for travellers.

The series of warnings on Thursday affected flights between Italy, Ireland, the UK, Spain, Bulgaria, Romania, Poland, Finland, Austria, Denmark, Belgium, Portugal, France, Greece, Serbia and Czechia.

The threats affected flights by Ryanair, Aer Lingus, Wizz Air, Finnair, easyJet, Aegean Airlines, Iberia, Sky Express, Air Serbia and LOT Polish Airlines. All landed safely. Searches of the planes found nothing suspicious.

In the UK, Heathrow, London’s principal airport, and Birmingham airport received threats overnight, but it was determined that they were hoaxes with the help of specialist investigators from the Metropolitan police’s aviation police command.

The Met said threats had been made against four aircraft departing from or arriving at Heathrow. A police spokesperson added that, after an assessment, “no security issues [were] identified”.

The day of hoaxes followed similar threats on Tuesday, when seven planes across Europe were searched or diverted after malicious alerts. On Monday a flight from the UK to Bulgaria was diverted after a threat that was apparently without substance. In total at least 33 flights have been affected this week.

Investigators said on Thursday there remained no evidence that the hoaxes were being orchestrated by a hostile terror group or state actor, such as Russia or Iran, although inquiries across Europe into the incidents continue.

The global airlines body Iata condemned the disruption and urged authorities to identify the origin of the threats. “Safety is the number one priority for the industry and we take all threats seriously,” said Nick Careen, Iata’s senior vice-president for operations, safety and security, in a statement.

The EU transport commissioner, Apostolos Tzitzikostas, said it was too early to know if the incidents were linked but that any threats against civilians were unacceptable.

Bomb threats emailed to airports in Rome and Sofia were signed “fighters of the Male State”, according to sources at Aeroporti di Roma, which manages the city’s two airports, and a text seen by Agence France-Presse.

Male State is a Russian far-right, ultranationalist and misogynistic movement founded in 2016 by Vladislav Pozdnyakov. On his Telegram channel Pozdnyakov, a prominent supporter of the war in Ukraine, did not comment on or claim responsibility for the bomb threats.

The emails reportedly read: “We, the fighters of the Male State, will make history with our actions today. Your feminist regime will remember this for ever. The explosion will occur in 60 minutes. Today, your skies will turn red.”

A Cork airport spokesperson confirmed to the Guardian that the airport had received an anonymous threat by phone on Thursday morning, causing a flight to be delayed. It was understood to be a Ryanair flight from London Stansted.

A separate Ryanair flight from Pescara in Italy to Brussels-Charleroi was inspected after the word “bomb” was discovered scrawled on the walls of its onboard toilet.

The affected flights had knock-on effects but were a tiny fraction of the 27,400 flights scheduled in Europe on Thursday, according to the European air traffic control agency, Eurocontrol.

The pilot of an Aer Lingus flight from Dublin to Rome that diverted to Évreux was being questioned by French authorities for initially refusing to land at a military base, according to French media reports.

The disruption followed cyber-attacks this week against supermarket refrigeration systems.

A Kremlin spokesperson said on Thursday it was “absurd” to blame Vladimir Putin’s administration for the fridge hacks. “We have ​such baseless accusations against our country, implicating it in everything,” Dmitry Peskov told reporters.

In Rome, Guido Crosetto, the Italian defence minister, said: “It seems to me that every day now, from refrigerators to blood supplies in France, to these fake alarms, there’s someone who has an interest in stoking tensions.”

Asked if he knew who was behind the hoaxes, he said: “It’s called ‘under-threshold warfare’ because it’s below the attribution threshold. It’s done that way on purpose, so no one can pin the blame.”

Many of the affected flights were operated by low-cost carriers but there was little discernible pattern surrounding the incidents.

Aviation experts say fake threats occur daily but that such a number of credible threats, even if malicious hoaxes, appears to point to coordinated action.

While getting a large explosive onboard should be ruled out by multiple layers of security and screening, the effects of any detonation would be multiplied for planes at typical cruising altitude of 35,000ft.

Diversions are expensive as well as highly disruptive, but airlines have little choice in the face of any remotely credible threat.

The ready availability of airline operations information on the internet means that any hoaxer now has a level of detail about the schedules, the type of plane and its technical specifications that would have eluded all but insiders decades ago.

Rhys Jones, an analyst at Osprey Flight Solutions, an aviation intelligence firm, said the confidence of airlines in European airport security should not mean any change to normal protocols after the hoaxes, but “one of the natural responses will be to see double-downed security, media coverage and public concern about travelling”.

He said the threats seemed linked but “a reasonably chaotic campaign with no particular region targeted”, and while they could weaken future security responses through the normalisation of threats, simply creating disruption could be regarded as a success by those carrying out the hoaxes.

Additional reporting by Pjotr Sauer in Amsterdam and Angela Giuffrida in Rome

#106. Accused double murderer gives evidence in high-profile case – as it happened

Beau Lamarre-Condon tells double murder trial he went through Jesse Baird’s phone and poured water into his fuel tank

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The court has adjourned for the day. Here are some of the key moments from Lamarre-Condon’s evidence today.

The former police officer took the witness box as the first defence witness.

He became emotional as he spoke about his experience working for NSW police. Lamarre-Condon said he experienced bullying and torment at work and told the court of instances of homophobic comments from his superiors.

Lamarre-Condon told the court he poured water into the fuel tank of Baird’s vehicle in what he believed to be a “tit-for-tat”.

He alleged his experiences at work made him feel suicidal and his voice wavered as he spoke of the situation.

Lamarre-Condon said he read messages on Baird’s phone and accessed it while he was in the shower. He also told the court he looked through the device while Baird was sleeping and was chased out of Baird’s home.

That’s where we’ll leave our coverage for today. The trial will continue tomorrow morning.

Lamarre-Condon has told the court about an occasion when he attended the home of his friend – known to the court under the pseudonym Fiona Rosso.

At the time, he was commencing beginner acting classes and he asked Rosso if she could film with him.

According to Lamarre-Condon, they were rehearsing a “serious play” where the friend’s character wanted to go to the police, while the character Lamarre-Condon was playing did not.

Lamarre-Condon tells the court that he jokingly asked Rosso, “You would help me hide a body right,” and she responded, “Yes of course I would mate.”

double quotation markIt was joking banter in line with the play.

Lamarre-Condon tells the court the “banter” was something officers joke about:

double quotation markWhen you have a heavy suitcase, you might joke, ‘shit how many bodies are in here?’

It was not serious and now I would never, ever try to joke about that … if I could go back in time, I would never joke about those things ever again.

Lamarre-Condon afraid he would lose job over medication

Lamarre-Condon tells the court he wanted to start taking medication that belonged to his stepfather, who was prescribed medication for depression and anxiety:

double quotation markI was very fearful that I would lose my job as a police officer if I was prescribed any medication.

Lamarre-Condon says one of his superiors said that if he “finds out anyone is on medication, that we would not be able to keep our job”.

He tells the court he made a complaint about this.

Lamarre-Condon tried to avoid Paddington after breakup with Baird

Lamarre-Condon tells the court he tried not to go to Paddington after their breakup in November 2023:

double quotation markI did not want to see Jesse, did not want to run into Jesse.

Lamarre-Condon tells court he could have obtained a gun from youth gang member

Lamarre-Condon says his work in the youth command meant he could have gotten his own gun:

double quotation markI work with youth gangs. Some of their families are in the Comanchero … I could easily go to these kids that look up to me ‘here’s four grand … go get me a gun’, and have a gun six hours later.

Shortly after Baird asked Lamarre-Condon to refrain from contacting him in November 2023, Lamarre-Condon says he saw scratches on the rims of his Tesla.

double quotation markI parked so far away from the curb, I believed it was Jesse who did this.

He says his mother had seen Baird’s car earlier that day.

double quotation markI know tit-for-tat isn’t right, I did go to the street where Jesse parked his car and did pour water into the fuel tank.

Lamarre-Condon has told the court he posted a bouquet of flowers on to his story around mid-November 2023 while Baird was in Melbourne.

He says it was to give the impression it was from Baird and wanted to see if another man Baird was seeing would message him and say, “well I’m also dating Jesse”.

Asked if he had gone through Baird’s phone around this time, Lamarre-Condon tells the court he looked at Baird’s messages with that other man while the TV presenter was in the shower.

Baird later called Lamarre-Condon and asked if he went through his phone to block someone:

double quotation markI denied, denied, denied … within 10 hours of denying it I essentially came clean.

He and Baird went back and forth about the flower messages, he tells the court:

double quotation markI told Jesse the flowers were sent to my mum by her partner … that wasn’t true.

I then sent a voice not by text message, saying yes I did block [the man] on your phone, that was to try to insinuate they were from you when they weren’t.

I completely owned up to it, I was truthful and honest 10 hours later.

Lamarre-Condon tells the jury his experience at work made him feel suicidal:

double quotation markAll the constant pushback, the bullying, torment, being told I can go back to picking up dead bodies … [it] made me feel suicidal. I didn’t want to be at work any more.

His voice begins shaking as he describes how he felt:

double quotation markAs a youth engagement officer, I have a personal relationship with these young offenders.

Me not showing up to work is not like showing up to a general duties [shift] … This is me letting down young people that needed me as a role model, needed me to help them, needed me to be there for them.

It was too much for me and it made me feel like having thoughts to take my life.

Lamarre-Condon tells the court he tried to take his own life, picking up tissues and wiping his face as he gives his evidence.

Crisis support services can be reached 24 hours a day: Lifeline 13 11 14; Suicide Call Back Service 1300 659 467; Kids Helpline 1800 55 1800; MensLine Australia 1300 78 99 78; Beyond Blue 1300 22 4636

Lamarre-Condon says he was having problems at work in 2023 after getting pushback on a proposal to run equestrian therapy for women.

When he escalated the issue beyond his bosses, Lamarre-Condon says direct supervisors were not happy:

double quotation markThey pretty much told me, if I wanted to involve them I can go back to picking up dead bodies on the street and not [go] back to youth command.

Lamarre-Condon says Baird did not realise it was him and began to gave chase.

double quotation markHe was screaming out to who he believed was a random saying, ‘Let’s go cunt you’re lucky I don’t have a fucking bat or a knife with me.’

When Lamarre-Condon realised he still had Baird’s wallet and phone, he threw it back at Baird.

The wallet fell on the footpath and the phone went down the drain, he tells the court.

Lamarre-Condon tells court about a night Baird left key out for him

Lamarre-Condon says he and Baird had a conversation about staying the night at the Paddington home on 17 August 2023 and he agreed to leaving the key in the letterbox.

He tells the court he arrived after 10pm, opened the back door and left a bobby pin in the front door.

double quotation markI have a split-second decision: do I sit here and have a conversation about why I was on his phone … or do I run?

He coincidentally saw Baird that day and took the video of the screen, before sending him the footage.

Lamarre-Condon says sexual relationship with Baird recommenced after birthday dinner

double quotation markI did see Jesse, it was a very normal and amicable conversation … We had a normal conversation about normal things.

Defence lawyer John Stratton SC asks: “Was there an occasion where you went to Jesse’s house and saw him through the bedroom window?”

double quotation markI saw Jesse having sex with a man I did not know.

I left … and actually laughed about it with friends … we were no longer together …

Lamarre-Condon tells the court this happened on only one occasion.

Lamarre-Condon tells court Baird told him he ‘wasn’t ready for a relationship with anyone’

double quotation markI told him this made me feel a bit upset because we were moving towards – I guess being a couple.

Jesse was honest with me [and] became emotional and started crying, said he wasn’t ready for a relationship with anyone not just me.

Lamarre-Condon said he was looking for something more serious and needed a few weeks away to “let my romantic emotions settle”.

Lamarre-Condon says the situation was “very amicable”.

Lamarre-Condon tells the court it was “common” for Baird to leave his key out for him:

double quotation markI let myself in and I was waiting for him to come home from work.

A message sent from Baird to Lamarre-Condon, and read to the court, said: “Key is in the letter box for you.”

Lamarre-Condon tells court about using Baird’s phone

The former officer says in January 2023 he and Baird went to an escape room. Baird then gave Lamarre-Condon access to his phone and asked him to edit a video of them together at the escape room, Lamarre-Condon tells the court.

#102. Gavin Newsom commutes sentences of all death row inmates in California

Gavin Newsom, a Democrat and a rumored presidential candidate, moved to commute the death sentences of 548 inmates in California for one of his last acts as governor of the state.

California Governor Gavin Newsom said Thursday that he will change all death sentences in the state to life in prison without the possibility of parole.

"I take this action because I firmly believe that state-sanctioned killing is incompatible with California's values, undermines the foundations of our legal system, and diminishes each of us," Newsom said in a letter to Californians.

There are 548 people on death row in California.

The outgoing governor added in the letter that he has long opposed the death penalty and issued a moratorium on executions in 2019.

The California state constitution requires the California Supreme Court's approval to grant clemency to anyone convicted of a felony more than once, which means the court would have to approve 382 of those commutations, or roughly 70%, according to numbers provided by Newsom's office.

Newsom's announcement also comes after the failed execution of Christa Pike renewed scrutiny of the death penalty.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Newsom, who is rumored to be considering a run for president in 2028, said the decision was among the most difficult choices of his career because it will surface painful memories for crime victims' families.

"As Governor, I alone bear responsibility for this decision and its impact, just as I alone would bear responsibility for each execution," Newsom wrote.

He acknowledged that Californians had voted to keep the death penalty. In 2016, 53% of people rejected a ballot measure that would have abolished the death penalty in the state.

"But the constitution also gave every governor clemency authority, and that power exists for moments when law as applied cannot be squared with justice," Newsom said. "For me, this is that moment."

The American Civil Liberties Union (ACLU), who has said the implementation of the death penalty reflects racial bias, applauded the announcement.

"These commutations reflect what justice demands: an end to a racist, error-prone, and inhumane punishment that has burdened California for far too long," said Anthony Romero, the executive director of ACLU.

In 2025, 17 countries carried out a total of at least 2,707 executions, according to Amnesty International.

#100. Australians are yet to see the benefits of AI and datacentres – and US midterms show they could be political kryptonite

Albanese should tread carefully as he struggles to make case for how the new technology will benefit ordinary voters

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

When will Australia see the benefits of the AI and datacentre boom? So far, all we’ve seen is the possibility of changes to copyright that would hurt Australian creatives, special rules about power and water use, and the threat of mass displacement or replacement of workers, with the trade-off being a mere 10% chance of human extinction.

Now add the Reserve Bank’s assessment this week that datacentres were part of growing “inflationary pressures” when lifting interest rates to an 18-year high.

Warnings about looming negatives have been coming for months, but even the RBA has no idea when to expect the positives. And, as the results of the US midterms show us, datacentres are becoming political kryptonite for voters not sold on the AI boom, meaning the Albanese government should tread carefully.

As Labor mulls its long-awaited plan to attract AI investment, this week’s RBA decision demands politicians better explain what the benefits of this rollout will be, and why it is worth the (hopefully short-term) pain. It also requires the government to consider other inflation responses, beyond the RBA.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

The governor, Michele Bullock, warned “some pain now will prevent even worse pain” in the future. But why should ordinary workers endure pain that is not of their own making?

Some of the richest companies in the world are helping heat the economy to such an extent that Bullock flagged it in her opening remarks, just moments after pointing to global oil prices, low productivity and expectations of prolonged inflation.

“Parts of demand have also been stronger than expected. In particular, investment has been much stronger than expected, including due to datacentre-related investment,” she said. “This has added to inflationary pressures by adding to demand at a time when the domestic economy is already capacity constrained.”

The RBA’s statement of monetary policy highlighted that “AI-related investment is driving rapid growth in prices for technology-related goods” in its second paragraph.

But asked when AI would start having a “positive impact on inflation”, Bullock shrugged “your guess is as good as mine”.

She said “if productivity improves dramatically because of AI” – “if”, not “when” – then we would see faster growth, but also warned it could increase the “neutral interest rate”.

“So if people think that productivity improvements will help to lower inflation and keep interest rates low, that’s not necessarily the case,” Bullock said.

Even the nation’s chief economic wonk is hazy on when the benefits start flowing.

These aren’t nimby arguments against datacentres or luddite concerns about technological growth; there are serious economic questions to be considered here, and social ones already being examined worldwide.

Deloitte Access Economics on Thursday reported Australia’s $1.39tn investment pipeline included 37 datacentres worth $141bn – a 36% increase in a year.

Deloitte director Sheraan Underwood said the datacentre boom was “competing directly with housing and infrastructure for workers and materials” and that Australia had “less room to absorb” the investment surge thanks to high inflation.

Deloitte reported “the economic dividend may take time”, predicting “substantial” construction gains but that the “direct economic payoff” wouldn’t be as big. The technology fit-out is largely imported goods and few workers are needed to actually staff the facilities.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

Of course the datacentre boom will support AI takeup, greater automation and productivity, but Deloitte warned: “Construction demand and inflation pressures are arriving now, while the productivity dividend may come much later.”

After RBA warnings about demand, much media focus has naturally turned to government spending. Press gallery pundits have zeroed in, particularly, on green subsidies and home batteries; the shadow energy minister, Dan Tehan, blasted a “blowout” from a budgeted $2.3bn to $8.5bn.

That’s $6.2bn more than budgeted. Or, in another way, 4% of the total $141bn datacentre investment.

Or 16% of the growth in datacentre investment in just one year.

Economics and finance, like politics, is a series of choices. If everything is on the table for savings, as Labor has said, shouldn’t we consider the benefits of untrammelled investment in AI factories before we start slashing social programs achieving good?

This week’s datacentre backlash in the United States is instructive. Politico recently reported polling showing half of Americans oppose datacentres near their homes; NBC exit polls found 56% of voters said AI and datacentres were “crucial” or “major” factors in the midterms. Datacentres were also literally on the ballot in dozens of towns, with public votes overwhelmingly won by proposals to restrict construction, according to vote trackers.

The Australian backlash is much smaller, but growing. Community groups are organising and protesting. Labor should not overlook bubbling voter unease.

So what to do instead? Beyond leaving it to the “blunt instrument” of monetary policy for the RBA to smash the economy, there are other options Labor could consider. They are novel, but the times demand novel solutions.

Senator David Pocock has proposed a Senate committee to investigate “alternatives” to monetary policy, suggesting options including “temporary, progressive” changes to income tax or superannuation. Independent economist Saul Eslake has said a small rise in superannuation rates, from even 12% to 13%, would take some money out of workers’ pockets and help curb spending. Pocock, again, suggested taxes on windfall profits and gas exports to take more cash out of the economy.

Of course we need new AI and datacentre investment, particularly as the government looks for a magic bullet to end stagnant productivity. But the government has so far struggled to make the case for how this new technology will benefit ordinary Australians, who are left with a feeling of uncertainty and a hole in their back pocket.

→ Baca artikel asli

#99. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#96. ANC under pressure to reverse South Africa decline

More than 500 parties are contesting the elections

South Africans are voting in highly contested local elections likely to see a haemorrhaging of votes for the country's biggest party, the African National Congress (ANC).

This year's voting season has been marked by intense campaigns that have included politicians spinning cars, watching grannies knit and even wading through giant-sized potholes.

Election officials say voting is going smoothly, despite long queues at some polling stations.

Leaders are campaigning to head South Africa's nearly 300 municipalities, responsible for basic service provision such as water, electricity and refuse collection.

For the ANC, the elections are a chance to prove it still has what it takes to govern. The party had a disastrous showing in the last national election, when it lost its parliamentary majority for the first time since 1994 when the racist apartheid system ended.

Support for the party has been dropping significantly over the years because of anger over high levels of corruption, unemployment and crime.

At its peak, in 2004, the ANC got 70% of the vote in the general election. Two decades later its share dropped to 40%.

Polls and pundits have predicted a further loss in these elections but President Cyril Ramaphosa has denied that his party was in a downward spiral.

"What I'm seeing here is that the ANC is going higher and higher and on the 4th [of November] we will show those naysayers," he told supporters at the party's final election rally on Sunday.

In townships like Alexandra, north-east of Johannesburg, the ANC has traditionally had a strong supporter base but poor governance and voter apathy are unravelling this support.

The BBC visited the sprawling township a week before the elections and spoke to residents to get their views. One of them was Sanele Ntombela, who is a first-time voter excited to make his voice heard.

Sanele Ntombela (left), seen here with his friends Tshiamo Mbange (centre) and Lwandle Dladla, is excited to cast his vote

We found the 18-year-old hanging out with his friends on a street corner. Most of his friends said they had no intention of voting and had not registered to do so.

They cited a lack of change and deteriorating delivery of basic services.

But Ntombela was looking forward to voting for the first time and thought it might lead to change.

"We face many challenges in Alexandra. There's rubbish everywhere and we struggle with electricity and water and many other issues," he said.

Just metres away, heaps of rubbish lay gathered beneath a streetlight pole covered in election posters from the different political parties.

On the other side of the street, drain water trickles through the many potholes that have gutted that part of the road.

The ANC faces immense pressure to reverse years of decline

The BBC also spoke to Zandile Mncube, who said she wouldn't vote, even though she is registered to do so.

The 33-year-old woman also cited poor service delivery and performance by local leaders for her decision.

"It makes no difference [whether I vote or not]. Things have gotten worse over the years," she said.

Alex, as the township is known, is a stone's throw away from Sandton, Africa's richest square mile, but the differences could not be starker.

Sandton is known for its skyscrapers, designer shops and well-maintained roads but in Alex, houses are sandwiched together and rubbish, either piled together or left strewn across the pavement, is everywhere. The stench of raw sewage and uncollected rubbish is heavy in the air, but residents appear used to it.

The issues plaguing Alexandra are not unique. A drive around Gauteng province reveals similar patterns of neglect and disrepair, especially in the townships.

South of Johannesburg, Soweto looks marginally cleaner than Alexandra, but residents also complain about service delivery.

Mduduzi Makhubo, 45, has lived in Soweto his entire life and he told the BBC of the community's daily struggles with accessing water and electricity.

"Every Monday we know we won't have water after 2pm," he said.

Makhubo is registered to vote and intends to use his vote to deal with the "arrogance" he says local leaders have towards residents and "change the status quo".

Can this man broaden the appeal of a South African party seen by some as 'too white'?

South Africa's political marriage of convenience avoids divorce - just

These areas offer a small, but important, glimpse into the state of South Africa's local governance.

Years of reports by the auditor-general have highlighted the dire state of most of South Africa's 257 municipalities.

A recent report only gave 39 clean audits - the highest ranking a public entity can achieve - between 2024 and 2025. None of Gauteng's three metro cities, Johannesburg, Tshwane and Ekurhuleni, featured in this list.

Earlier this year auditor-general Tsakani Maluleke said Johannesburg "needs ongoing attention".

"If we don't that, we are going to continue on this slide where financial health deteriorates [and] where service delivery continues to be compromised, which is what we are living," she told lawmakers.

The city has been labelled "effectively bankrupt" due to the dire state of its finances. Dirty streets, unstable electricity supply and persistent water issues have been common for years.

Experts the BBC spoke to agreed that all eyes will be on Africa's wealthiest city as well as the country's seven other biggest municipalities, or metros, particularly those in Gauteng when the results come in.

Of South Africa's eight metros, only three are governed by a single party. In Gauteng, all three metros are governed by coalition governments, with the ANC the largest party.

Political analyst Melanie Verwoerd said the elections would be a "verdict ... and judgment" on the ANC.

"And the expectation is that the ANC generally in the country is going to [perform] the worst it's ever done," she said.

Several polls point to a continued decline in support for the party in Wednesday's election, contested by over 500 political parties.

One, conducted by Ipsos, put the ANC's support at 30%, down from the 46% it got at the last local elections in 2021.

The same poll, along with others, points to a slight increase in electoral support for the Democratic Alliance (DA), especially in Johannesburg. Ipsos predicts the DA will garner 25% support in this election, up from 22% last time.

DA leader Helen Zille has been vying for the mayoral chain in the city and her unusual campaign has attracted a lot of attention.

The former Cape Town mayor has been captured swimming in crater-sized potholes, ziplining over sinkholes and riding into her final rally in the city in a classic BMW 325i, known locally as a "Gusheshe".

She believes the DA is the country's best choice for a government, telling local radio broadcaster 702 last week that "towns and cities that work best are under DA governance".

Verwoerd, a former ANC MP and South Africa's ambassador to Ireland, predicted that coalitions would continue being the order of the day across Gauteng.

These types of arrangements have often been viewed as unstable and chaotic at a local level and the constant change in leadership, usually caused by parties falling out with each other, has affected service delivery in many municipalities.

Zandile Mncube says things have got worse in her township over the years

Verwoerd said in her view, the most stable coalitions would be those that replicate what the ANC and DA have done on a national level, where the historic rivals have agreed to share power.

Reflecting on Cape Town, she said it was unlikely that the DA would lose control of the municipality. The coastal city is widely considered the country's best-performing metro and has been run by the DA since the late 2000s when it unseated the ANC.

Ethekwini, which includes the port city of Durban in KwaZulu-Natal, would be a "different story", Verwoerd said.

The uMkhonto weSizwe (MK) Party has been projected to perform well in the area but it is unclear if it will secure the outright majority needed to run the metro. Polls suggest it won't and will need partners to form a government.

The party was founded by former South African President Jacob Zuma and was the biggest party across his home province of KwaZulu-Natal in the 2024 provincial election.

Verwoerd reflected that while various options were on the table for the party, forming a government would be "very messy" given its refusal to work with either the ANC or DA.

A confident Zuma, addressing supporters on Sunday, vowed to take control of a number of municipalities after the elections.

Another political analyst Dr Levy Ndou echoed Verwoerd's assessment, saying that a coalition between the DA and ANC seemed unavoidable in most metros.

"We're not sure whether the arrangement of the government of national unity could have the potential to influence how the metros are going to be run," he said.

Both experts also spoke about the declining voter turnout in South Africa's elections. Over 29 million citizens are registered to vote, but the turnout, especially among young people, has seen a steady decline.

Ndou attributed this to the "calibre" of the candidates campaigning and the lack of visibility of local councillors, who are often the ones elected to lead their neighbourhoods, in election campaigns.

South Africa's electoral commission has seven days to announce the results, but parties have 14 days to form a governing structure.

#95. Austrian tycoon Rene Benko sentenced to 2 years in prison for fraud

Austrian businessman Rene Benko, who suffered a major fall from grace after his real estate empire, Signa Holding, went bust, was handed two more years in prison.

Rene Benko, an Austrian businessman whose legacy as a property tycoon collapsed with the fall of his real estate empire Signa Holding three years ago, was sentenced to two more years in prison on Thursday.

The Innsbruck Regional Court found Beko guilty of defrauding €5 million from the private foundation of Austrian industrialist Hans Peter Haselsteiner. The payment from Haselsteiner arrived just weeks before Signa Holding declared insolvency.

It was the third criminal proceeding against Banko, coming after two earlier convictions. He was sentenced in the first case in October 2025 on charges of insolvency-related fraud. In September 2026, he received a 30-month term for harming creditors.

The new sentence against Benko, 49, comes just ‌three weeks ⁠after ⁠he was released to house arrest to serve out a previous conviction.

Benko's lawyer said he would assess whether to appeal the conviction over the weekend.

Benko was acquitted on a second charge that he had hidden a luxury rifle worth €80,000 from creditors.

Benko was once thought to have been a wunderkind for having established himself as a self-made entrepreneur after dropping out of college.

At its peak, Signa Holdings accumulated a portfolio worth more than $30 billion, with stakes in New York's Chrysler Building and Berlin's KaDeWe department store.

In 2023, Signa Holdings declared bankruptcy, dealing a blow to a myriad of European property investors and others.

The fall of Signa was considered the single biggest financial earthquake to have hit the European property market since the global financial crisis of 2008.

#94. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#91. Why a US-funded vaccine trial on babies was cancelled in Guinea-Bissau

Like many neighbouring countries, Guinea-Bissau waits until babies are six weeks old to vaccinate them against hepatitis B, while the WHO says it should be done at birth (file photo)

A proposed trial of a hepatitis B vaccine on newborn babies in Guinea-Bissau has been cancelled following accusations that the trial was "unethical" and "neocolonial".

The trial was funded by the US, under Health Secretary Robert F Kennedy Jr, who has a long record of expressing scepticism about vaccines.

Police have seized a batch of vaccines from the research centre at the heart of the controversy.

The proposed trial drew international criticism because it planned to withhold a vaccine recommended by the World Health Organization (WHO) from half of the participating newborns.

Some public health experts argued that the proposed trial raised broader questions about whether vulnerable populations in African countries were being asked to take risks that researchers would not accept in wealthier countries.

The BBC has asked the US department of health and human services for comment.

The study was planned at the Bandim Health Project, a research centre that has operated in Guinea-Bissau since 1978.

It was designed to involve 14,000 newborns, with half receiving a hepatitis B vaccine shortly after birth and the other half not being given the shot.

Bandim said the trial aimed to investigate whether the hepatitis B vaccine given at birth has broader health effects beyond preventing infection.

Previous studies in The Gambia and Guinea-Bissau have raised questions about a possible link to increased mortality.

Researchers argued that because Guinea-Bissau does not currently offer routine hepatitis B vaccinations at birth, despite WHO recommendations, they have a unique opportunity to study its effects before such vaccines are introduced in 2028.

But critics questioned the ethics of withholding the vaccine from some babies, particularly in one of the world's poorest countries, where the infection is widespread.

The Trump administration funded the proposed study, with the CDC awarding the University of Southern Denmark (SDU) $1.6m (Â£1.2m) in December 2025.

Walter Kazadi Mulombo, the WHO representative in Guinea-Bissau, told the BBC they became concerned after receiving questions about the study and finding no publicly available protocol.

He said the main ethical concern was that the trial would withhold an intervention already proven to protect newborns against hepatitis B.

"The vaccine has been used for more than 30 years in more than 115 countries," he said. "A study that will withhold that intervention from other newborns is unethical."

He warned that babies could be exposed to the virus during childbirth if their mothers were infected, as well as later in life.

Nearly a fifth of the population of Guinea-Bissau are infected with hepatitis B, a viral infection that affects the liver.

Those infected during infancy are particularly likely to develop chronic infections, which kill up to 25% of people, mostly through liver failure or liver cancer, according to data published in 2020.

The WHO is working with the government to bring forward the birth-dose vaccine.

"The country had been expected to add it to its routine immunisation schedule in 2028, but officials are considering introducing it in the first quarter of next year," Mulombo said.

Guinea-Bissau currently vaccinates children against hepatitis B at six weeks, which means it is less effective.

Guinea-Bissau's government prohibited hepatitis B vaccine trials involving children in January. At the time, it requested a team of experts from the Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention (Africa CDC) to help evaluate the study.

Health Minister Quinhin Nantote said in October that the suspension remained in force and had not been overturned by the Council of Ministers after reports emerged claiming that the trial would be taking place after all.

"We were surprised to learn from media reports that the study might proceed," Nantote said. "We are outraged because even I, as the minister responsible for this sector, was not informed."

The dispute intensified after the SDU said a revised study protocol had received ethical approval "from the relevant authorities in Guinea-Bissau", which Nantote denied.

Dr Gavin Yamey, a professor of global health and public policy at Duke University in the US, criticised the proposal.

"The proposed trial reeks of neocolonialism," he told the BBC, questioning why the research was not being conducted in Denmark.

His criticism centred on the decision to withhold a recommended vaccine from some newborns in a country where hepatitis B is widespread.

SDU dean Jakob Grauslund said the trial had never started, no babies had been enrolled and no vaccinations administered as part of the research.

The SDU said it had received a separate, positive assessment from the university's own research ethics committee.

Grauslund said the university had sought an additional independent assessment, although Danish law did not require one.

He added that the relevant Danish statutory research ethics bodies were unable to undertake the review.

Following the approval from the SDU's ethics committee, Grauslund decided that the project could proceed.

He, however, stressed that this did not replace the separate authorisation required from Guinea-Bissau's authorities.

On 19 October, Guinea-Bissau's Judicial Police searched the Bandim Health Project's facilities in the capital, Bissau, and seized a batch of hepatitis B vaccines.

Police said the batch was intended for vaccinating children but did not disclose how many doses had been seized or the specific grounds for the investigation.

They stressed that the seizure did not, in itself, mean the vaccines were defective or unsafe.

The police have not explicitly said whether their investigation relates to the proposed trial, the importation of the vaccines or other circumstances surrounding the batch.

Grauslund said he could not comment on the seizure of the vaccines.

The proposed trial was funded by the US government at a time of significant changes to vaccine policy under President Donald Trump and Health Secretary Kennedy.

Under Kennedy's leadership, a US vaccine panel attempted to end a longstanding recommendation that all newborns receive a hepatitis B vaccine at birth, but a federal judge later blocked this, along with other changes to vaccine policy made by the Trump administration.

Critics have questioned whether the research could help support that policy shift, although the study's stated aim was to investigate potential effects of the vaccine beyond preventing infection.

Kennedy has praised the Danish researchers as "the deities of vaccine research", and cited earlier findings from their work in support of concerns about vaccine safety. A subsequent study by some of the same researchers, however, found no evidence of the previously reported effect.

There is no evidence that the proposed trial was designed to justify a change in US policy.

#89. A bitter end for Ronaldo - the icon who transformed Portugal

Cristiano Ronaldo's old rival Leo Messi left Argentina less than a month ago with a nation applauding him.

The Portugal legend has gone another way, and it was always going to be the case.

The 41-year-old announced his international retirement after 23 years but also after a fallout with coach Jorge Jesus which was resolved with a sanction of 30 days, that had already been served, and a fine of 255 euros.

It was the punishment imposed on the Portuguese striker for leaving the team's training camp without permission. Jesus kept him on the bench for one match and said he did not plan to start him in the next, so Ronaldo went home.

His last appearance came on 24 September against Wales. He ends his international career with 146 goals in 234 games.

A message went around Portugal with a big thank you for everything, calling Ronaldo a global ambassador and an inspiration to millions. Even so, the way it ended leaves a bit of a bitter taste.

Was he going to be named in the next squad? Possibly, as Jesus said the door was open and the decision was Ronaldo's. What is clear is that he was no longer an automatic starter, and Cristiano has never handled that well.

So is this another hissy fit? Partly, yes. He reacted the way he always has when a coach questions his place. But the label hides how much he adapted over the years.

Such a pattern appears with almost every man who sat on the Portuguese bench - there were clashes, but also communion with managers and team-mates that help explain this ending.

Six tournaments, 11 goals - Ronaldo's World Cup legacy

Ronaldo's most iconic moments as he retires from international football

What legacy does Cristiano Ronaldo leave on the international stage?

When a young Ronaldo first joined the squad, he arrived at camp with his baseball cap on backwards, enormous sunglasses and headphones turned up so loud they could be heard from a distance.

He would not take the cap off to eat among veterans like Luis Figo and Rui Costa. Luiz Felipe Scolari saw an extraordinary player who was immature and obsessed with showing off. He reprimanded him in public, then told him privately that if he changed his attitude, he would one day captain Portugal.

The first great disappointment came early, and it came at home. At 19, Ronaldo played in the final of Euro 2004 at the Estadio da Luz in Lisbon, in front of a country that had convinced itself the trophy was already on its way.

A single headed goal by Angelos Charisteas was enough for unfancied Greece to win it by surprise. When the final whistle went, Ronaldo could not hold back the tears, and the image of the teenager crying on his own pitch travelled everywhere.

It was a painful way to learn what a final costs, and it left him with a hunger that never really went away.

Figo owned the number 7 at the time, so Ronaldo had to have 17. Figo helped him settle but had no intention of making it easy. When he left the national team in 2006, the shirt and the team became Ronaldo's.

In September 2005, before a qualifier in Russia, Scolari and Figo went to his room to tell him his father had died. Scolari offered to send him home. Ronaldo chose to play, asked his team-mates to behave as normal, and spent time in the dressing room doing ball tricks.

That was his way to cope with the expected but still hugely hurtful passing of his alcoholic dad. He later called Scolari "father". That way the national team had become the place that held him together.

Things went sour with Carlos Queiroz. After the 2010 World Cup exit against Spain, asked to explain a poor performance, Ronaldo told journalists to ask the coach. His influence in the camp had grown so large it changed routines and habits, which explains why the coach felt undermined.

The sociologist Joao Nuno Coelho offers the best lens on how Portugal sees him. In his analysis, the national team became a representation of Portuguese identity and of the country's need to be seen internationally.

In years of economic hardship and a crisis of confidence, a team that won admiration abroad was important beyond football.

Ronaldo stood at the centre of that need and created contradictory feelings. Some Portuguese questioned his individualism and his ego, and not everyone saw themselves in his personality.

Coelho explains criticising him became more difficult as time went by. The country wanted to be proud of him and sometimes felt uneasy at the same time.

The former international Jorge Andrade told me some supporters preferred figures like Figo, Eusebio, Paulo Futre or Rui Costa. Even so, Portugal was proud to have him. And with the titles, the doubts eased.

Under Fernando Santos in 2016, he changed. He accepted playing as a centre forward next to Nani - he had never taken a coach's instructions so naturally.

After the opening draw with Iceland, he told Nani in private that he trusted Santos, and the dressing room began to see a real captain. After missing a penalty against Austria, he barely slept. Santos woke up and found him already awake.

At the training camp in Marcoussis, he took a fan's megaphone and told supporters Portugal had won nothing yet. He was enjoying the leadership role.

In the final in Paris, he left the pitch in tears, injured. He had not had the best tournament, with a muscle complaint that did not let him perform at his best.

Nani took the armband. At half-time Ronaldo pulled himself together to encourage his team-mates, and before extra time, still limping, he told Eder he would score.

But the old instinct never fully disappeared. During extra-time, Ronaldo stood in the technical area giving orders until the fourth official sent him back. Other coaches I consulted found it excessive.

His greatest strength, the need to influence every result, also made limits hard to accept.

When he debuted in 2003, Portugal was still searching for its first major title. Some players mark an era, and others change a national team for good. He belongs to the second group.

That night in July 2016 gave Portugal its first European Championship.

Three years later he scored a hat-trick against Switzerland to put them into the first Nations League final, which Portugal won. In 2025 he lifted a second Nations League at an age when most players have retired.

No one has scored more goals for a national side, and no one has pulled on the Portugal shirt more times.

Under his influence, Portugal stopped being a gifted team and became one that arrives at every tournament expecting to contend, while a whole generation of players came through a dressing room where he decided what the standard was.

Coelho's point holds here as well: a small country found a way to be noticed, and for two decades he was its face.

After 2016 the question around him changed. Fewer asked whether he was selfish, and more asked whether Portugal depended on him too much.

Ronaldo scored 146 goals and won 234 caps for Portugal

In the World Cup in 2018, he scored three against Spain, including a free-kick after 45 attempts in major tournaments, and the team still went out to Uruguay.

After a difficult 2026 World Cup, much was made of him still starting up front when in fact statistically that was his best period.

Under Roberto Martinez, with 28 goals in 37 games, 0.756 per match, improves the 0.624 with Santos.

The federation has announced a special tribute to honour his contribution, and that is necessary as the exit has not left fans room for a celebration they want to be part of.

Not only they grew up with him, he took them to places they had not yet been.

A bitter end for Ronaldo - the icon who transformed Portugal

#87. One Nation asks donors at $3,000-a-head Sydney fundraiser to accept ‘non-disclosure agreement’

Transparency experts raise concerns about plans to use NDA to gag attendees at event featuring Pauline Hanson in lead-up to NSW election

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

One Nation is attempting to gag donors from ever revealing details of discussions at an invite-only, cocktail-dress fundraiser with the party’s senior leadership team, in a move described as a “worrying precedent” for democracy.

Invitations to the “very special evening” at an undisclosed central Sydney venue next Thursday show donors will be charged $3,000 for a “private and very discrete” four-hour event.

Donors have been told they will be meeting with Pauline Hanson; One Nation’s national director, James Ashby; federal MP Barnaby Joyce; the federal party’s chief of staff, Chris Taylor; and its New South Wales leader, Mike Newman.

But unlike at other One Nation fundraisers – including a $2,000 invite-only event in Melbourne in September – this time donors have been asked to abide by a “non-disclosure agreement” preventing them from disclosing the identities of any attendees. They are also stopped from sharing the nature of any discussions at the event, which is reportedly set to feature a “cocktail and canapé service throughout the evening as the sun sets” and a live auction.

“Do not disclose information about what occurs at the event, including conversations, activities, photographs, video or other details obtained through attending,” says the agreement, which is contained in an invitation seen by Guardian Australia.

“Do not provide information, photographs, recordings or comments about the event to journalists, media organisations, bloggers or other publishers.”

The NDA states that these obligations “continue after the event has concluded”.

These type of fundraising dinners are common and frequently organised by Labor and Liberal parties. But senior sources from both major parties involved in running such events say the use of non-disclosure agreements is unprecedented.

Transparency International Australia’s chief executive, Clancy Moore, has been a long-term critic of closed-door fundraising dinners and believes they offer “cash for access”. He said the use of non-disclosure agreements was a worrying new step.

“Charging guests for a political fundraiser and requiring them to keep quiet about it raises serious questions about transparency ahead of an important state election,” Moore said.

“Voters deserve to know whether any political commitments are being made behind closed doors to paying guests. A non-disclosure agreement should never be used to shield political fundraising from public scrutiny.”

Christian Slattery, a senior campaigner at the Australian Democracy Network, said Australians were “rightly suspicious of exclusive cash-for-access events”.

“Slapping an NDA on a political fundraiser sets a worrying precedent. Democracy thrives in the sunlight,” Slattery said.

One Nation has disclosed $5,000 in reportable donations so far in 2026-27, across three donations for a fundraising dinner with Joyce, according to disclosures with the NSW Electoral Commission.

In that time, disclosures indicate the NSW Labor party has raised $286,200 in total reportable donations, while the NSW Liberals have raised $95,900.

The individual threshold for a reportable donation in New South Wales is $1,000.

A now defunct webpage containing details of Thursday’s event had stated that registered attendees would receive a text message with its location just an hour before the start time.

In early September, the address of a three-course fundraising lunch with Hanson in Melbourne was leaked to the media, leading to journalists photographing donors as they entered the venue.

Other high-profile fundraisers in the state have been targeted by protesters, leading to clashes with One Nation supporters and a heavy police presence.

One Nation has also required candidates at state elections to sign non-disclosure agreements preventing them from discussing their potential candidacy with the media or revealing confidential information obtained during the process.

The fundraiser comes amid a major donations drive by the party in NSW, where it was polling at 25% of the first-preference vote, ahead of the Coalition on 24% and behind Labor on 29%, according to a Sydney Morning Herald Resolve poll in September. Polling conducted internally by Labor suggests One Nation could pick up more than 20 seats in the state’s lower house.

Newman and Joyce hosted several publicly advertised, small-ticket dinners and forums in October, with more events planned in November.

#86. Would you take a test to see if your life will be cut short? I did - and it changed me

Laura Sides faced a dilemma of whether to get tested to see if she had inherited her father's condition

If you could find out if you had a gene that would cut your life short, would you?

This is the dilemma Laura Sides faced when she was offered a test for the rare form of early onset dementia that her dad was diagnosed with in his 40s.

Laura spoke to Dr Sian Williams, host of BBC Radio 4's Life Changing podcast, about her decision to take the test and how it has shaped the rest of her life.

Growing up, Laura says she always felt close to her dad as they had a "similar nature". He was a GP and "the most amazing doting father," she says. When his Alzheimer's symptoms became more severe, Laura dropped out of university to care for him.

"We did have a really lovely time," she says, remembering trips to a lighthouse and collecting coasters. "I'd run him baths and just play music and pour jugs of hot water over his head and he loved it."

Laura had just celebrated her 21st birthday when she began caring for her dad

It was at a hospital appointment with her dad when a doctor told Laura she had a 50/50 chance of developing the same type of early onset Alzheimer's known as the "London variant".

"All I could think was, that's a toss of a coin," she says. "I was still so involved with what was going on with him [her dad]. I just pushed it really, really deep down."

Laura's dad died in 2009, just a year after her mum had died.

"All of a sudden I got such an overwhelming urge to leave," she says. So she booked a solo around-the-world trip, eventually settling in Melbourne, Australia.

It was there that thoughts of inheriting her dad's condition "started creeping in" and she went to see a specialist in 2013.

He told her a test could determine with 99.999% certainty if she would inherit early onset dementia. If she had the gene, her expected age of decline would be 46 to 48, he said.

"I just thought, I'm not there yet. I don't want to do that."

'Toss of a coinâ if Iâd inherit dementia gene

As Laura entered her 30s she found herself thinking about the test more and more and decided she wanted to know. But first, she had to undergo six months of genetic counselling.

"There's not many realms in life where you find out your fate and it can be very difficult information to process," she says.

After a six-week wait for the test results to come through, the day finally arrived. Laura says she felt "extremely nervous".

She had booked a table for lunch with a friend afterwards - but went into the appointment alone.

"I said, 'Let's cut straight to it.' They said, 'We're really sorry, it's not good news, and you have inherited the gene.'"

She remembers "almost a white blurry noise of just sitting there".

"I came out of that room and my friend burst into hysterical tears." But Laura didn't react. "We went for our lunch and it was lovely. I don't know if it'd sunk in."

The reality hit later. "I was angry. I felt it was so unjust. I was just too confused at how I was meant to live now. How do you live with that knowledge?"

Laura has taken part in medical trials all over the world

Laura began to feel a pull back home to the UK, especially as her sister was pregnant. "It doesn't make sense... to live on the other side of the world when she's going to have a baby."

However, her around-the-world travels continued as she took part in medical trials before and after her diagnosis.

"It felt like such a positive to be involved in something out of such a negative situation."

Laura (right) is a chef and owns a cafe restaurant and catering business with two friends

Laura is now 43 - three years away from the age doctors predicted she will begin to notice symptoms.

"I keep waiting for something to sort of hit home. I guess one day I'll start declining but that doesn't keep me up at night."

She is "grateful and so lucky for the people in her life". But meeting anyone new is hard.

"Do I tell them? Do I not tell them? At what point do I tell them?

"And more often than not, when I have told them, it ends."

However she says she has been "proven wrong".

"I have been shown I can be loved despite this. It fills my heart with reassurance and joy".

Laura says she had always "naively assumed" she would be a mother.

"[Its] one of the very few things that I never questioned that I'd be good at and I wanted to do. That's hard... but it doesn't make sense to do it. It wouldn't be fair."

Laura loves swimming and is aiming to swim in as many seas as possible

For Laura, life is now about tapping in to things that "make her happy".

"It's not about huge aspirations. It's not like I want to see the Grand Canyon... I want to have time now around the people that I adore and make me feel safe.

"I love nature. I love being outside. I'm obsessed with being submerged in water. It sounds so corny, but...I believe it really is actually the little things are the big things.

"I hope I just have a wonderful few years in this world... drinking champagne and eating oysters - two of my favourite things."

If you or someone you know has been affected by dementia, you can access support from the BBC Action Line.
