#32. Melania Trump calls for lasting Ukraine truce as she hosts Russian and Ukrainian officials

Watch: US First Lady Melania Trump given Ukraine's highest award for women

US First Lady Melania Trump called for a "more enduring" ceasefire in Ukraine as she welcomed Russian and Ukrainian delegations at the White House following last week's truce in the southern region of Kherson.

Hundreds of residents of the Russian-occupied Ukrainian town of Oleshky facing starvation and constant bombardment were able to evacuate during a six-day ceasefire, which both Moscow and Kyiv credited Trump with brokering.

"The lives of innocent people must transcend the division of war. I hope Oleshky inspires greater peace," she said.

The unusual White House event saw senior Ukrainian and Russian officials meet away from the setting of official US-brokered peace talks they have been holding without success since last year.

The delegations were made up of Ukraine's human rights ombudsman Dmytro Lubinets and his Russian counterpart Yana Lantratova, as well as Ukrainian MP Davyd Arakhamia and Kremlin envoy Kirill Dmitriev.

Melania Trump was credited by all for playing a key though unspecified role in the ceasefire, and all parties conveyed to her the gratitude of the Russian and Ukrainian presidents.

Melania Trump handed Dmitriev and Arakhamia "personal letters" for their respective presidents

The US first lady said she had "reviewed demining plans, aerial images, assessed roadways, and discussed evacuation routes, hospital access, vehicles, and medical resources" during the truce.

"Last week in Oleshky and neighbouring areas of Kherson, people experienced the simple joys of life for the first time since 2022," Trump said. "They walked down familiar streets without fear of explosions."

She thanked both Volodymyr Zelensky and Vladimir Putin, making no reference to the origins of the war, which began when the Russian president launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, and which has caused thousands of civilian casualties and the displacement of millions.

Dmitriev, a businessman and Kremlin special envoy who has previously represented Moscow during peace talks with Ukraine, thanked Trump for "saving and touching so many lives".

"Let us continue making a humanitarian contribution together; this is within our power," Dmitriev said, before gifting Melania Trump a framed drawing by an 11-year-old Oleshky evacuee.

He also praised Trump for her work on reuniting children separated from their families by the war. These include children allegedly abducted by Russia.

Arakhamia, a Ukrainian MP who heads Zelensky's party in parliament who has also been part of the Ukrainian negotiating team, said the Oleshky ceasefire had constituted the "biggest de-escalation since 2022".

"We are creating a momentum where both sides are learning theâ¦ mechanics of how to actually keep the peace when the peace comes," he added.

He acknowledged that the "political track" of negotiations to end the conflict was "complex" but said Ukrainians were asking Melania Trump "to keep going and find new missions and de-escalate more."

At the end of the event, Trump handed Dmitriev and Arakhamia two letters to be hand-delivered to their respective presidents.

The White House later said Melania Trump had invited her guests to view two historic artifacts which she had "personally selected as powerful symbols of peace".

One was an 18th Century medicine chest which was removed from the White House when the British set it on fire in 1812. "Once adversaries, the United States and Great Britain ultimately established a strong alliance," the White House said.

The second artifact was a cup gifted in 1899 to the French ambassador in recognition of his efforts to bring the Spanish-American war to an end.

Melania Trump has generally remained discreet on foreign policy matters, and neither Moscow nor Kyiv have elaborated on the nature or extent of her involvement in coordinating the delicate ceasefire - the most enduring and widespread of the war so far.

A source who works in the local government of the Kherson region told the BBC that work on the Oleshky evacuation had been ongoing for almost a year.

Oleshky evacuees arrived in Ukraine after a journey through southern Russia and Belarus

However, the source said, "at a certain point other individuals became involved, seemingly after most of the groundwork had already been laid and subsequently began taking credit for the progress that had been made."

It was difficult to determine exactly who had played what role and at which stage of the process, the source acknowledged, adding that "those who had been directly involved in the negotiations for nearly a year were eventually prohibited from communicating publicly about the matter."

Speaking after the meeting in the White House had taken place, President Zelensky said the success of the Oleshky ceasefire "had proved a very simple thing: when Putin wants the fighting to stop, it stops."

"The same applies to our people who are held in Russian captivity. When Putin wants, they go home."

A source close to American officials shared a similar assessment, implying to the BBC that despite the outward praise, "the person who made [the ceasefire] possible, whether we like it or not, is essentially Putin."

"And this was done in order to pass the ball to [Donald] Trump... so that it would have some positive effect for his midterm elections."

There has been no change whatsoever in Russia's position, Ukrainian political analyst Dmytro Levus told the BBC.

Moscow "agreed to it because there was pressure from the American side... In doing so, the Russians confirmed Zelenskyy's words that, when necessary, it turns out Russia can quickly stop firing and even maintain that ceasefire."

As the frontline region of Kherson experienced its first six days of peace in several years, the rest of Ukraine continued to come under Russian attack.

This week, several drone and missile attacks killed and injured civilians across the country. On Monday alone, more than 40 people were killed in a single Russian strike on an aid distribution centre in the region of Zaporizhzhia. More are thought to still be buried under the rubble.

Additional reporting by Anastasiia Levchenko in Kyiv

#30. Mother and daughter killed in grizzly bear attack, Canadian authorities confirm

Conservation officers say more than one bear may have been involved in attack on women near their home

Search teams in Canada have discovered the body of a 20-year-old woman and confirmed that both she and her mother were killed in an attack that conservation officers now believe could have involved multiple grizzly bears.

Late on Tuesday, conservation officers in British Columbia said the two women, who were reported missing over the weekend, were located near a trail a short distance from their home.

The discovery has shaken a community where, earlier in the year, a grizzly attacked a group of schoolchildren and their teachers.

“Our thoughts are with the families of the victims during this tragic time,” Inspector Jeff Tyre of the British Columbia Conservation Officer Service said in a video posted online. Tyre later said the conservation service was investigating the possibility that more than one bear was involved in the fatal attack.

“We appreciate people’s concerns and the desire to assist but it is critical the public stay out of the area – both due to the danger and to allow officers to do their work,” he said.

Jenny Balcarras, an art therapist, and her daughter Selah Reed were reported missing by relatives over the weekend. A family member posted on Facebook about them, later updating it after the conservation service announced the suspected attack to indicate that their vehicle had been found at a trailhead near Bella Coola, British Columbia. Balcarras’s remains were found first and Reed was located a day later.

A fundraiser for Balcarras started by her family mourned a woman who had recently moved to Bella Coola, a community of roughly 2,100 people, for a new job and “to start a new chapter” of her life. She leaves behind three adult children and a grandchild.

“[Jenny] was working toward building a future and had not yet had the opportunity to establish savings or put aside funds to help her family with unexpected expenses such as these,” wrote relative Mary Melissa Smith. “Tragically, no one could have anticipated that her family would be faced with such an unimaginable loss.”

The family also mourned 20-year-old Selah, who had her “whole life ahead of her” before the attack.

“She was only beginning her journey into adulthood, making this loss even more heartbreaking,” wrote Smith.

Both Balcarras and Reed had lived in communities in remote and rugged parts of the province, as well as time in Alaska.

The suspected attack happened at the Snooka West trail area, about 8km (5 miles) east of Bella Coola, on the province’s central coast.

Bella Coola, which sits at the end of a fjord along the province’s central coast, is a remote location, and abundant food sources mean that grizzly bears are a common sight in the region, which is informally known as the Great Bear rainforest.

Grizzly attacks in the province are exceedingly rare, but last November, 11 people, some as young as nine years old, were injured when a grizzly emerged from the forest near 4 Mile, a Nuxalk community, and attacked a school group on a lunch break alongside a walking trail. Despite an extensive search, the bear was never located.

Samuel Schooner, elected chief of the Nuxalk Nation, said on Tuesday: “Our thoughts are with the victim’s family as they learn of this tragic event.” Schooner said a trapping effort to find the bear was under way and asked media to refrain from contacting community members.

The province’s premier-designate, Lorne Doerkson, called the news “heartbreaking” and said he was thinking of the victims’ family and the Nuxalk Nation.

“At a time of such profound grief and uncertainty, my thoughts are with everyone who is hurting and with all those supporting one another through an incredibly difficult day.”

#29. France needs 'more public services', 'salaries are too low', 'schools are in very, very bad shape'

As France confronts another wave of strikes and protests, Gaëlle Martinez, National Secretary of the trade union Solidaires, presents the general strike as more than a confrontation over wages, public spending or the cost of living. Her account reveals a deeper struggle over economic dignity, the future of public services and the democratic right to collective protest. “People just want to live, not survive.” For Martinez, deteriorating schools, insufficient salaries and the dismantling of public services are not isolated policy failures. They are interconnected symptoms of political and economic priorities that, in her assessment, place fiscal restraint above social welfare. Her demand for “a new budget” that is “not based on cuts” is accompanied by a call to “tax the biggest companies, the richest companies where the profits are made.”

#27. Major tech stocks plunge as US midterms add to fears of AI slowdown

Hundreds of billions of dollars in market value has been wiped from major tech firms in a stock market rout after the US midterm elections sparked concerns of a slowdown in AI development.

Following Democratic wins after election campaigns run on slowing AI, shares in firms across the tech and artificial intelligence sectors plummeted.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq composite stock index closed down by more than 5.5% to lose an estimated $2.5tn in market capitalisation, according to Dow Jones data, while other key US stock indexes also saw big losses in their worst days since 2025.

The sell-off comes on top of broader concerns about whether the huge amounts being spent on AI will pay off.

Democrats in races across the US campaigned to slow the onslaught of data centres and AI, and there were dozens of measures on ballots around the country aimed at restricting the development of the hubs crucial for powering the technology.

Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa campaigned heavily against data centres, pledging a moratorium on new construction in the sector.

Shares in companies heavily exposed to AI data centres, including crucial chipmakers and semiconductor manufacturers, fell sharply on Thursday, highlighting investor concerns that had already been growing.

Nvidia shares slid more than 9%, knocking more than $400bn off the firm's market value.

Other chipmakers also suffered, with AMD and Micron both dropping by more than 17%. Intel shares also dropped, down 10.3%, while memory card maker SanDisk plunged more than 22%.

The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index, which tracks the performance of 30 of the largest companies involved in semiconductor production, fell by more than 12%.

Shares in Elon Musk's companies SpaceX and Tesla also plunged, falling 8% and nearly 11% respectively and shaving more than $300bn off the firms' market value on Thursday.

Musk had spent nearly $450m backing Republican campaigns, including at least $45m in support of Ken Paxton for the Texas senate seat, but Texas was turned blue in the Democratic tsunami on Tuesday night.

In total, the so-called magnificent seven tech companies, including Apple, Nvidia and Tesla, have shed a trillion dollars in value in a single trading day.

The stock falls also hit other major US stock indexes. The S&P 500 index fell by 4.4% by the end of trading, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average erased gains made on Wednesday to close down more than 3.4%.

The Nasdaq Composite fall was so large it is now in what market watchers call correction territory, which is where an index or company value falls at least 10% below its recent high.

Live: What can Democrats do with control of Congress? We answer your US midterms questions

Food, fuel and taxes â can the Democrats actually make life cheaper?

Boebert, billionaires' tax and other midterms races still to watch

Susannah Streeter, chief investment strategist at Wealth Club, said the steep sell-off on the Nasdaq and the S&P 500 was sending shockwaves into other markets.

"Fresh panic over the AI trade has hit Wall Street hard," she said.

"Fears that the vast sums poured into AI infrastructure may not deliver the returns investors have been banking on are now colliding with the prospect of a sharp slowdown in data centre construction."

Some tech leaders have said they were caught off guard by the backlash against data centres in the midterms.

OpenAI president and co-founder Greg Brockman said he believed everyone had been surprised by the reaction to data centres, and more work needed to be done to ensure communities were on board.

"A lot of our shift over the course of this year has really been to realise that from San Francisco trying to build infrastructure in Texas, Georgia, and other places, you can't just tweet and say: 'This is going to be good.' You need to be in those communities," Brockman said at the WSJ Tech conference in Napa, California on Wednesday evening.

Meta's president Dina Powell McCormick said that the industry needed to put more effort into selling AI as a good idea.

"I think we need to double and triple down on telling the story," she told the WSJ conference.

Alongside the immediate impacts of the US mid-term elections, some investors have become concerned that AI shares could be in a bubble that is due a correction.

#25. How elderly ‘unsung heroes’ powered Lula’s historic fightback in Brazil

Older voters praised for helping tip balance in leftwing president’s favour against far-right Flávio Bolsonaro in runoff

It had been nearly a decade since Celia de Oliveira Soares last voted, and months since the 101-year-old great-grandmother last left her home.

But on 25 October, when Brazil held what many considered its most important presidential election in decades, relatives helped Soares into a wheelchair and drove her to a nearby school to make sure the far-right candidate, Flávio Bolsonaro, did not prevail.

Soares clung to the banister as, step by step, she climbed two flights of stairs to the polling booth under the watchful eye of her granddaughter’s husband, Marcio Marques.

Inside, the frail but jaunty centenarian keyed the number “13” – representing Bolsonaro’s leftwing rival, President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva – into an electronic voting machine and pressed confirm.

“I like Lula. I really do!” enthused Soares, a retired bank employee who has lived in Rio de Janeiro most of her life.

“I don’t like Bolsonaro or his father. So the efficient thing to do was go out and vote,” Soares added, as her grandsons, Tito and Ravi, played on the balcony around her surrounded by pot plants and books.

Soares is one of more than 1 million elderly people who, having missed the election’s 4 October first round – which Lula lost – decided to vote in the second.

Never since the return of democracy in the 1980s had the first-round loser bounced back, but Lula did just that. And after Lula’s shock revival, elderly voters have been hailed as the election’s unsung heroes – including by Lula himself.

At his 81st birthday party last week, the leftist veteran shed tears while describing his gratitude to the elderly people who helped him clinch a historic fourth term while clutching walking frames and sticks.

“I honestly think it was the most powerful and human image I’ve ever seen during an election,” Lula said of viral pictures showing voters in their 80s, 90s and 100s being carried into polling centres by relatives or carers, some in stretchers or wheelchairs.

In north-east Brazil, where Lula was born into rural poverty in the 1940s, a 114-year-old retiree called Josefa Pereira da Conceição went to vote wearing a red T-shirt emblazoned with Lula’s face and the slogan “Strength of the people”.

Election experts are still crunching the numbers to fully understand how Lula pulled off his unprecedented fightback, finding 10.3m extra votes between the first and second rounds.

Explanations include a sharp increase in welfare benefits on the eve of the vote; an extraordinary digital mobilisation involving a pro-Lula WhatsApp group that grew from 50,000 to nearly 11m members in less than a month; and a student-led street rebellion in which millions of citizens protested across Brazil.

Some believe elderly people were also crucial, noting how 1.4 million of the 3 million extra voters who took part in the second round after skipping the first were over 70. Pre-election polls suggested such voters were more inclined to vote for Lula.

Speaking at Lula’s victory celebration, the former minister Simone Tebet called the result a cross-generational triumph which sent a message to the democratic world. “It is possible to resist fascism – all you need to do is mobilise society,” Tebet declared, claiming: “Young people hit the streets and golden-agers saved our democracy.”

The political journalist José Roberto de Toledo said there was concrete evidence of higher turnout among older voters in the runoff, particularly those aged 60 to 79. Voting is only mandatory in Brazil for those aged 18 to 69.

“The images [of elderly people voting] aren’t misleading: they reveal a phenomenon that isn’t just an isolated case. It’s not some made-up story or a staged example. It really happened – and it was a movement you can measure statistically,” Toledo said of the images suggesting an unusually large number of older voters.

That said, Toledo believed the swing to Lula was explained mostly by fear, not age. Voting data suggested many people who backed Lula in the runoff had previously voted for Bolsonaro’s rightwing adversaries, hoping to protest against Lula’s Worker’s party (PT), which has governed for nearly 18 of the last 24 years.

In the runoff, faced with a straight choice between Lula, a familiar and predictable figure even for detractors, and Bolsonaro, who represented a leap into the unknown, many preferred the former.

“A protest vote in the first round [became] a safety vote or a lower-risk vote in the second – even if it wasn’t a happy vote,” said Toledo, who believed Bolsonaro alienated many voters with his “arrogant” behaviour after winning the first round.

Particularly damaging was the rightwinger’s authoritarian-sounding vow to “re-democratise” the already democratic country – something Toledo suspected stoked fears that Bolsonaro aspired to be “the emperor of Brazil”.

Even before those remarks, Soares fretted over the direction Brazil might take under Flávio Bolsonaro, whose father, the former president Jair Bolsonaro, is serving a 27-year sentence for plotting to seize power after losing the 2022 election to Lula.

“His father wanted to stage a coup … and stay on as president, just like Getúlio [Vargas did in 1937],” recalled the great-grandmother, who was 12 at the time and lived through the subsequent eight years of dictatorial rule.

Marques, a 51-year-old food entrepreneur, said his grandmother-in-law was also critical of how Jair Bolsonaro undermined vaccination efforts during the Covid pandemic, when 700,000 Brazilians died.

“She’s got three great-grandchildren … and she understands she’s nearing the end of her life. But she’s worried about the country, about her family and about future generations,” said Marques, becoming visibly emotional as he recalled watching Soares exercise her democratic right, perhaps for the last time.

“It was so moving to see her strength and desire [to vote] … We felt she had an intuition, or something telling her she needed to be part of this … She doesn’t know if she’ll get another chance,” added Marques, whose 85-year-old father, Helço Muniz, also voted Lula – in his case for the first time.

Sitting in her armchair overlooking Rio’s Guanabara Bay, five days after the election, Soares voiced excitement that Lula would soon start his fourth and final term. “I’ve been fond of him ever since he was poor,” she said of the lathe operator turned president, who first took office as president in 2003. “He was poor and he became rich, but he carried on being a simple man.”

Occasionally, Soares’s memory betrayed her and she needed reminding that Lula had pulled off one of modern history’s greatest democratic comebacks. “But that’s wonderful!” she beamed.

Reminded of Lula’s feat, Soares predicted Bolsonaro would flee abroad.

“He’s already gone!” her granddaughter, Poliana Soares Jordão, 46, told her. “His dad’s in prison and he’s fled.”

Soares, who has lived through four coups, 16 presidential elections and four Lula wins, was not surprised. “He must have gone to the US, then,” the centenarian guessed, correctly. “He’d been getting ready for that for a while, hadn’t he?”

#23. Eight killed in series of shootings in western Turkey

The shootings took place in rural areas northeast of the city of Afyonkarahisar (pictured)

Eight people have been killed and one seriously injured in a series of shootings in western Turkey.

The attacks took place in the Afyonkarahisar province on Thursday, with victims found in several locations in three separate vehicles, according to local reports.

The suspect fled, triggering a manhunt, and authorities later said he had been arrested in EskiÅehir province.

CCTV footage published in Turkish media showed a man in a black jacket, white trousers and glasses clutching a long weapon as he stood by a red minivan.

Turkey's state-run Anadolu Agency reported that three people were killed near Ayazini village and three others near Kiyir village. In Yukaritandir, two women were found injured and later died in hospital.

Two teachers were reportedly among the dead.

Local media said roads leading to the affected villages were closed by police, while officers launched a large-scale operation to find the suspect.

The newspaper Milliyet reported that a vehicle allegedly taken by the attacker had been later found stuck in mud - the suspect having abandoned it before fleeing on foot.

In a statement posted on social media, the Afyonkarahisar Governorate said "all relevant institutions of our state have been working in coordination since the very first moment and are meticulously carrying out the necessary work".
